Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP150 
Proposal name: 
Promoting private sector investment through large scale 
adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for 
Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sectors of Bangladesh 
Accredited entity: 
Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL) 
Project/programme size: 
Medium 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Impact potential   
 
 
 
 
 Scale: Medium/High 
1. 
Bangladesh, in South Asia, forms the larger and eastern part of the Bengal region. It is 
situated in one of the largest deltas in the world, formed by a dense network of the 
distributaries of the rivers Ganges, Brahmaputra and Meghna, and more than 230 major rivers 
and their tributaries. The total land area of 147,570 square kilometres consists mostly of low 
and flat land. With a population exceeding 166.5 million, Bangladesh is ranked as one of the 
most densely populated countries in the world.  
2. 
Although its status is being reviewed, Bangladesh is currently regarded as a least 
developed country. The country’s economy recorded remarkable growth since 2010 as a result 
of strong private consumption and investment. The country’s real gross domestic product (GDP) 
grew from 7.3 per cent in 2017 to 8.2 per cent in 2019.  
3. 
Bangladesh is frequently cited as one of the countries most vulnerable to climate change 
because of its geographic location, the dominance of floodplains, and low elevation from the sea, 
among other things. The country experiences severe climate change impacts such as higher 
summer temperature, irregular monsoon and erratic rainfall patterns, floods and droughts, 
coastal and riverbank erosion, and salinity intrusion along the coast. 
4. 
Although the total greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions of Bangladesh amount to less than 
0.35 per cent of total annual global emissions, the country’s fossil fuel-related emissions have 
grown by 6.8 per cent since 2000. GHG emissions per capita increased from 0.18 tonnes of 
carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) in 1997 to 0.47 tCO2eq in 2014, with an average annual 
growth rate of 6 per cent. The increase was driven by rapid industrial expansion, and growth of 
energy production from fossil fuel sources.  
5. 
Bangladesh is the world’s second-largest apparel producer after China. Textile 
industries are one of the fastest growing and among the most important industries of the 
country. The sector’s contribution to GDP has grown from 3 per cent in 1991 to more than 13 
per cent in 2018 representing more than 80 per cent of the country’s annual exports. The textile 
industry (along with the garment industry) is one of the largest employers in the country, 
employing over 4 million people, or 6 per cent of the country’s industrial employment. 
6. 
The industrial sector in Bangladesh accounts for 47.8 per cent of commercial energy 
consumption, predominantly in the form of natural gas and electricity. However, the sector is 
not efficient in its energy use because of continuous usage of old or poorly maintained machines 
and inadequate energy management. The industries’ lack of interest in energy-efficient and 
conservation technologies is assessed to be due to inadequate financial incentives, and lack of

=== PAGE 163 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 160 
 
 
technical expertise. If the economic growth persists with the current industrial energy intensity, 
Bangladesh could face difficulties in managing the rising energy demand and consequently in 
achieving its GHG emission reduction targets under the Paris Agreement. 
7. 
Although energy-saving opportunities are present across the various industrial sectors, 
the textile sector (spinning, weaving, knitting and dyeing), and ready-made garment (RMG) 
sector (cutting, sewing, dyeing, ironing, and finishing) in particular are considered to have 
substantial scope for energy savings. The RMG sector is the most energy intensive of all industry 
sectors, consuming 15.4 per cent of the total industrial energy demand in Bangladesh. The 
textile sector follows the RMG subsector, with its demand representing 12.4 per cent of the 
industry sector.1 
8. 
Investment in energy efficiency is regarded by textile and RMG factories as an additional 
financial burden so is rarely undertaken, especially in small to medium-sized factories. The 
feasibility study conducted for the textile sector suggests that the larger textile facilities are 
typically 10–15 per cent more energy efficient than those in the small or medium categories, 
with efficiency differences being as high as 50 per cent in some cases. In the RMG sector, small 
factories having small-scale investments are more oriented towards increasing short-term 
profits than increasing productivity and profits in the long run. 
9. 
The proposed programme “Promoting private sector investment through large scale 
adoption of energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment 
(RMG) sectors of Bangladesh” offers concessional funding to local financial institutions to on-
lend to private sector textile and RMG entities to procure energy-saving equipment and 
technology for replacement or expansion with the aim of reducing GHG emissions from the 
subsectors, and ultimately from Bangladesh. The programme period is 20 years including an 
implementation period of 11 years. 
10. 
IDCOL of Bangladesh is the accredited entity (AE). In addition to IDCOL, four local 
financial institutions (LFIs) will be executing entities (EEs), BRAC Bank Limited, City Bank 
Limited, IDLC Finance Limited, and South East Bank Limited. The Sustainable and Renewable 
Energy Development Authority (SREDA) under Ministry of Power, Energy & Mineral Resources 
will be the implementing entity for the policy component. 
11. 
The programme is comprised of five components: two funding components and three 
technical assistance (TA) components. 
12. 
Component 1 entails USD 133.0 million of financing for energy-saving equipment and 
technologies for the textile sector. Under component 1, IDCOL, as the EE, will make GCF funds 
available to the private textile manufacturing businesses in the form of senior loans on 
concessional terms together with its own contribution of USD33.0 million, which IDCOL would 
provide from its own account or mobilize from other sources. Disbursements will be made from 
IDCOL in USD and local currency for the businesses to procure energy-efficient equipment and 
technologies. Technical eligibility criteria require a 15 per cent energy-saving potential for the 
equipment to be funded. Technical eligibility will be confirmed by IDCOL based on 
manufacturers’ catalogues or certifications, or energy auditors’ reports. Component 1 will 
finance replacement and expansion of existing equipment and facilities. The maximum amount 
of a single loan is set as USD 20 million. 
13. 
In component 2, TA amounting to USD 3.05 million is to be provided from GCF to 
support IDCOL’s funding activities in component 1. This will help IDCOL to establish an 
implementation strategy and build the capacity of IDCOL staff for efficient adoption of energy-
efficient technologies as well as effective monitoring, reporting and verification of energy 
 
1 Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 (2015) prepared by SREDA and the Power Division, 
under the Ministry of Power, Energy and Mineral Resources

=== PAGE 164 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 161 
 
 
savings and GHG emission reductions. Market awareness of energy-efficient equipment and 
technologies will be enhanced to generate a project pipeline and for swift implementation of the 
programme. Government agencies will be approached so that challenges identified during the 
programme can be shared to develop enabling policies and regulations for investments in 
energy efficiency in Bangladesh. 
14. 
Component 3 provides USD 200.0 million of financing for energy-saving equipment and 
technologies for the RMG sector. As EEs, the four LFIs will on-lend the GCF concessional funding 
in USD to private sector RMG businesses for investment in energy-saving equipment and 
technologies. IDCOL, as the AE, will channel the GCF funds to the four LFIs. The LFIs will provide 
an additional USD 50 million on their own account in both USD and local currency in total. 
Technical eligibility criteria require a 10 per cent energy-saving potential for the equipment to 
be funded. Similar to component 1, technical eligibility will be confirmed by the LFIs based on 
manufacturers’ catalogues or certifications, or energy auditors’ reports. Component 3 will 
finance replacement and expansion of existing equipment and facilities. The maximum amount 
of a single loan is set as USD 20 million. 
15. 
Component 4 is for TA amounting to USD 2.3 million to support the lending activities of 
the four LFIs in component 3, and to promote energy efficiency investments in the RMG sector. 
This component includes support for the EEs to undertake preparatory work for 
implementation, and capacity-building for EE staff and private sector entities in the RMG sector. 
Technical support for the EEs and private sector entities is also provided for monitoring, 
reporting and verification of the savings achieved and for programme implementation. In 
addition, this component envisions a technology fair to be held within three years of the start of 
implementation, to promote market awareness and access to EE technology. This knowledge 
dissemination event will cover both the textile and RMG subsectors. 
16. 
Component 5 is for TA amounting to USD 1.15 million to strengthen the regulatory and 
institutional framework at the national level to overcome the operational constraints related to 
implementing energy efficiency and conservation in the two subsectors in the country. It offers 
technical support for assessing the existing regulatory framework and preparing policies and 
strategies conducive to the promotion of energy efficiency in Bangladesh. It further includes 
capacity development and training of government officials and other stakeholders on energy 
efficiency measures and financing. The component will be implemented by IDCOL, as the EE, 
and SREDA. 
17. 
The programme requires total financing of USD 340.5 million, of which GCF is requested 
to provide a USD 250.0 million loan and USD 6.48 million TA grant. The balance of USD 84.02 
million is to be provided by IDCOL (a USD 33.0 million loan plus USD 1.0 million contribution in 
kind), the four LFIs (a USD 50.0 million loan) and SREDA (a USD 20,000 in-kind contribution). 
The GCF loans have a tenor of 20 years including a 5-year grace period. 
18. 
The AE estimates that the programme will reduce or avoid about 18.6 MtCO2eq of GHG 
emissions in total over the 20-year lifetime of the investments by offering financial and 
technical resources to private companies operating in the textile and RMG sectors to access 
investment opportunities for energy-saving technology upgrades. In that estimation, 13.1 
MtCO2eq, or 70 per cent of the programme’s target GHG emission reduction will be achieved in 
the RMG sector while 5.5 MtCO2eq or 30 per cent will be achieved in the textile sector. 
19. 
The estimation is made taking into account the emission factors of the major sources of 
energy (natural gas, diesel, electricity from grid) being consumed by factories, and emission 
savings that can be achieved by introducing energy efficiency technologies. The AE further 
estimates that 50 per cent of the total funding would be used by large factories. The rest would 
be directed to finance medium- and small-scale factories.

=== PAGE 165 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 162 
 
 
20. 
In response to a question from the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) on 
treatment of the existing equipment replaced with energy-efficient equipment funded by the 
programme, the AE clarified that the replaced equipment might be resold in the market if it is 
still considered to have economic lifetime and monetary value. The AE agreed to take into 
account the estimated GHG emissions that are likely to continue if the replaced equipment, 
which is less energy efficient, remains in operation for some time. The AE explained that it 
assumes that the existing equipment has a machine life of 10–15 years and could be replaced 
and resold at years 6–10. Subsequently, the equipment would be used for 4–5 years by the new 
buyer before it is fully retired. According to the AE, these ongoing emissions can be estimated to 
be 1.5 MtCO2eq and 2.6 MtCO2eq in the textile and RMG sectors, respectively. 
21. 
Accordingly, the AE estimates that the total amount of GHG emission savings the 
programme is expected to deliver amount to 14.5 MtCO2eq, of which the saving in the textile 
sector is 4.0 MtCO2eq (27.6 per cent), and that of the RMG sector is 10.5 MtCO2eq (72.4 per 
cent).  
22. 
The TAP assesses the impact potential of the programme to be medium/high. 
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
  
Scale:  Medium/High 
1.2.1. 
Potential for scaling up and replication 
23. 
The programme offers USD 416 million for concessional financing and technical support 
to IDCOL and the four LFIs to support investments in energy efficiency in the sectors. Long-term 
concessional financing will be required from GCF so that loans from IDCOL and the four LFIs to 
the sectors can be priced low enough so that the present value of the energy-efficient equipment 
can be at par with that of conventional equipment when financed with ordinary market-priced 
loans. In the Bangladesh market today, the cost of energy-efficient equipment is 20–25 per cent 
higher than conventional equipment.  
24. 
The TAP considers that the programme scalability and replicability are largely 
dependent on the availability of concessional financing. The bankability of energy efficiency 
investments cannot be established in the absence of concessional financing. The textile and RMG 
sectors contain more than 1,400 and 4,000 factories respectively, among which only a fraction 
operates energy efficiently. Therefore, if the programme is to upscale, the funding requirement 
would be large. Accordingly, the TAP considers the programme’s potential for scaling up and 
replication to be limited unless a large amount of concessional financing is secured. 
1.2.2. 
Potential for knowledge and learning  
25. 
The TA components of the programme will contribute to knowledge creation and 
dissemination about energy-efficient equipment and technologies and their benefits for the 
textile and RMG industries among key stakeholders in Bangladesh. Knowledge and lessons 
learned will be accumulated and shared through consultations and, more importantly, through 
the monitoring activities to be carried out with the assistance of consultants during 
implementation. 
1.2.3. 
Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment 
26. 
The programme provides components that will help develop the energy efficiency 
market for the textile and RMG sectors in Bangladesh. The loan components support the private 
companies in the sector to overcome the financial and technical hurdles to investing in energy-
efficient equipment and technologies. Technology fairs are planned in the programme design to

=== PAGE 166 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 163 
 
 
create an interface with the investors/users, and the manufacturers/suppliers of energy-
efficient equipment. Knowledge and lessons learned collected throughout the programme’s 
implementation will be shared among key stakeholders including financial institutions, 
industrial communities and government agencies. The programme may contribute to bring 
down the costs of the energy-efficient equipment by creating a new market for energy-efficient 
equipment and technology in these sectors. 
1.2.4. 
Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies 
27. 
The programme promotes capacity-building of the policymakers in fields related to 
energy efficiency in industry especially in the textile and RMG sectors in Bangladesh. 
Component 5 of the programme, to be implemented jointly by the AE and SREDA, offers 
technical support for assessing the existing regulatory framework and preparing policies and 
strategies conducive to promoting energy efficiency, as well as training of government officials 
and other stakeholders.  
28. 
The TAP views the paradigm shift potential of the programme as medium/high. 
1.3 
Sustainable development potential   
 
 Scale: Medium/High 
1.3.1. 
Environment co-benefits 
29. 
The programme will promote reduction in the use of fossil fuels (natural gas, diesel and 
coal) as energy sources in the sectors’ operations, thus contributing to improved air quality in 
Bangladesh. Energy consumption in the textile and RMG sectors is skewed towards use of 
natural gas with grid electricity having a minor share. Diesel is mostly used to generate 
electricity as a backup and its use depends on non-availability of grid electricity. Although large 
factories are equipped with captive natural gas power plants that supply electricity, small RMG 
factories rely heavily on diesel generation sets to fulfil their power needs. The share of diesel 
has increased significantly, by 2,440 per cent, while the shares of natural gas and grid electricity 
have declined by 22 per cent and 75 per cent, respectively. 
1.3.2. 
Economic co-benefits 
30. 
The programme will help the industry to improve production and capacity utilization 
and reduced production costs, which will lead to improved productivity and competitiveness. 
The industry contributes more than 13 per cent of the country’s GDP and 86 per cent of the 
country’s export. It also generates more than 65 per cent of the country’s industrial 
employment, employing over 4 million people of whom 80 per cent are women. In addition, the 
industry promotes development of small-scale industries, because 99 per cent of ancillary 
activities such as dyeing, printing and manufacturing zippers and labels are carried out locally.  
31. 
The programme contributes to environment and economic development of the country 
by enhancing energy efficiency in the industry which contributes more than 13 per cent to the 
nation’s GDP. The sector is also the largest sector of employment. Although the industry has a 
workforce comprising more than 80 per cent women, any benefits that the programme 
generates are likely be indirect. As such, the TAP considers the sustainable development 
potential of the programme as medium/high.

=== PAGE 167 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 164 
 
 
1.4 
Needs of the recipient   
 
 
 
 
        Scale: High 
1.4.1. 
Economic and social development level of the country and affected population 
32. 
Bangladesh remains a least developed country although its economy recorded 
remarkable growth since 2010 as a result of strong private consumption and investment.   
33. 
Bangladesh is one of the most adversely affected countries in terms of climate change. It 
is listed in the top 10 most affected countries of the world in the Climate Risk Index for 2017. 
The topography is flat, and the majority of the landmass lies within 10 metres above mean sea 
level. All land types except highlands are exposed to monsoon flooding for part or all of the year. 
Floodplains located in the north-western, central, south-central and north-eastern regions are 
subject to regular flooding at different frequencies and intensity. The coastal plain is subject to 
cyclones and storm surges, salinity intrusion and coastal inundation. It is estimated that the 
impact of climate change on Bangladesh could result in an annual loss of 2 per cent of GDP by 
2050 and 9.4 per cent of GDP by 2100.  
1.4.2. 
Absence of alternative sources of financing 
34. 
The programme requires total financing of USD 340.5 million, of which a USD 250.0 
million loan and a USD 6.48 million TA grant are required from GCF. The GCF loans will be 
priced at a concessional rate and have a tenor of 20 years including a 5-year grace period. 
35. 
The public sector investments in Bangladesh are presently focused more on supply-side 
interventions in order to increase generation capacity and bridge supply gaps. Budgetary 
resources available to drive public sector investments for demand-side interventions, especially 
towards energy-saving technologies, are therefore constrained. 
36. 
Under the current market scenario, the industry may access commercial loans at an 
interest rate of 9–15 per cent based on creditworthiness and credit history. Although bank 
lending to the sector has grown by over 15 per cent since 2016, the sector is still perceived as 
being a high credit risk. There are several loan facilities for energy efficiency and conservation 
promotion; however, higher lending interest rates and the time required for appraisal have 
hampered smooth implementation of loan projects.  
37. 
The TAP notes that alternative sources of financing are not currently available, 
especially of this size and tenor. 
1.4.3. 
Needs of strengthening institutions and implementing capacity 
38. 
Although the Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 (2015) 
prepared by SREDA and the Power Division under the Ministry of Power, Energy and Mineral 
Resources  confirms the potential and importance of energy efficiency in the industry and 
building sector for economic development, the implementation and promotion of energy-
efficient equipment and technologies has been slow in Bangladesh due to lack of concessional 
funding and technical capacity. The programme includes capacity-building activities not only for 
financial institutions and industry but also for policymakers in fields related to energy efficiency 
in industry, especially in the textile and RMG sectors. Technical support is included for 
preparing policies and strategies conducive to promoting energy efficiency, as well as training of 
government officials. 
39. 
The TAP assesses the needs of the recipient of the programme as high.

=== PAGE 168 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 165 
 
 
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
 Scale: Medium/High 
1.5.1. 
Existence of a national climate strategy 
40. 
Bangladesh submitted its intended nationally determined contribution to the United 
Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in September 2015. Bangladesh 
ratified the Paris Agreement on 21 September 2016 and its nationally determined contribution 
(NDC) can now be found on the UNFCCC’s interim NDC Registry.  
41. 
Bangladesh’s NDC describes its plans for reducing GHG emissions through a progressive 
approach to developing its economy on a low-carbon pathway. In the NDC Bangladesh 
committed to reducing GHG emissions in the power, industry and transport sectors by 5 per 
cent below the business as usual scenario by 2030 using only domestic resources, or by 15 per 
cent below the business as usual scenario by 2030 if sufficient and appropriate international 
support is received. 
42. 
Bangladesh’s NDC identified three key emitting sectors: the industrial sector accounts 
for 40.6 per cent of total emissions; the power sector accounts for 32.8 per cent; and 
transportation 26.6 per cent. Accordingly, improving industrial energy efficiency is identified as 
one of the three key mitigation interventions together with renewable energy expansion, and 
transport infrastructure modernization. This has been embedded in the country’s 5-year 
planning process. 
1.5.2. 
Coherence with existing policies 
43. 
The Energy Efficiency and Conservation Master Plan up to 2030 (2015) contemplates a 
programme to extend low-interest loans for energy efficiency and conservation undertakings in 
industrial and building sectors. The programme will contribute to the government’s target to 
achieve a 10 per cent reduction in energy consumption by the industry sector by 2030, and 20 
per cent improvement in energy intensity by 2030. 
1.5.3. 
Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver 
44. 
IDCOL is the AE of this programme. It is also the EE of all the financial and technical 
components except component 3 where the four LFIs extend loans to the RMG sector. IDCOL 
will assess the eligibility of the borrowers in the textile sector for financing under the 
programme as per the criteria set prior to loan execution. 
45. 
IDCOL is a non-banking institution established in 1997 and is owned by the Government 
of Bangladesh. It has a mission to catalyse and optimize private sector participation in 
promotion, development and financing of infrastructure, as well as renewable energy and 
energy efficiency projects in a sustainable manner through public–private partnership 
initiatives.  
46. 
The financial performance of IDCOL is compatible with or better than other non-banking 
institutions in Bangladesh. The total assets of IDCOL increased from 66,980 million Bangladeshi 
taka (BDT)2 in 2015 to BDT 90,876 million in 2019 (i.e. by 35.7 per cent) while its loan book 
grew from BDT 36,964 million to BDT 68,799 million (86.1 per cent). Its total revenue increased 
from BDT 4,324 million to BDT 5,378 million (24.4 per cent) during the same period. It is rated 
AA1 by Credit Rating Agency of Bangladesh. The IDCOL loan portfolio in 2018 included 
renewable energy projects (33.2 per cent) and infrastructure (including energy efficiency) 
projects (66.9 per cent).  
 
2 As of October 2020, 1 USD = 84.70 BDT

=== PAGE 169 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 166 
 
 
47. 
IDCOL has implemented loans and grants amounting to more than USD 2,100 million 
channelled through the government from development finance institutions. Its development 
partners include World Bank, Asian Development Bank, Japan International Cooperation 
Agency, KfW, Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit, Inter-American 
Development Bank, United States Agency for International Development and GPOBA (now 
known as Global Partnership for Results-Based Approaches, GPRBA). Under the USD 100 million 
Energy Efficiency and Conservation Promotion Financing Project funded by the Japan 
International Cooperation Agency, IDCOL has approved USD 42 million for the RMG and cement 
sectors, out of which USD 25 million has been already disbursed.  
48. 
Component 3 will be executed by four LFIs: BRAC Bank Limited, City Bank Limited, IDLC 
Finance Limited and South East Bank Limited. 
49. 
BRAC Bank Limited, established in 2001, is well known for its banking services aimed at 
small and medium-sized enterprises in Bangladesh, with half of its lending portfolio being small 
and medium companies. In 2015-2019, revenue and net profit after tax increased from BDT 
6,835 million and BDT 2,436 million to BDT 9,948million and BDT 5,646 million (average 
growth rate of 11.4 per cent and 32.9 per cent), respectively. Total assets of BRAC Bank Limited 
amount to BDT 367,920 million in 2019 showing an average growth rate of 16.0 per cent during 
the same period, while its portfolio of loans and advances grew by 19.8 per cent per annum. In 
2018 it reported BDT 12,000 million of financing for green projects. The bank is rated as AA1 by 
the Credit Rating Agency of Bangladesh. 
50. 
City Bank Limited was established as Bangladesh’s first private commercial bank in 
1983. It provides a wide range of deposit and loan products and solutions catering for the needs 
of its broad client base. The total assets of City Bank increased at an annual growth rate of 14.8 
per cent since 2014to reach BDT 324,780 million in 2018. In 2019 it reported that its loan 
portfolio and profit after tax had increased to BDT 246,944 million and BDT 2,472 million with 
an annual growth rate of 6.7 and 22.5 per cent, respectively. The total assets of City Bank were 
reported to be BDT 354,698 million in 2019. In 2018 its lending amount to the RMG sector was 
BDT 39,251 million, and cumulative disbursements to green projects amounted to BDT 1,359 
million. It is rated as AA2 by Credit Rating Agency of Bangladesh. 
51. 
IDLC Finance Limited, established as a single product lease company in 1985, is 
regarded as one of the pioneers in the non-banking sector in Bangladesh. It currently has a 
diversified operation across different segments in banking services. Revenue and net profit after 
tax in 2019 amounted to BDT 4,815 million and BDT 1,522 million, exhibiting a growth rate of 
7.7 per cent and 5.7 per cent since 2014, respectively. During the same period total assets 
increased from BDT 57,160 million to BDT 114,655 million at a rate of 14.9 per cent. IDLC 
Finance Limited offers 52 green products under eight categories as defined by Bangladesh Bank, 
the Central Bank of Bangladesh. In 2018, the green banking portfolio stood at BDT 648 million 
and cumulative disbursements in the green segment reached BDT 1,255 million. IDLC Finance 
Limited is rated AAA. 
52. 
South East Bank Limited is a private commercial bank launched in 1995. It provides 
conventional banking products and services to retail and business clients, along with specialized 
products/services such as an investment fund and automatic fraud monitoring. Its total assets 
in 2018 were reported to be BDT 381.095 million, which was the result of annual growth of 13.6 
per cent over the previous four years. Revenue was BDT 14,724 million and net profit after tax 
was BDT 2,456 million in 2018. Its exposure to the RMG sector was BDT 50,850 million in 2018. 
The total lending to green projects was BDT 12,671 million. Southeast Bank Limited is rated AA 
by Credit Rating Information and Services Limited of Bangladesh.

=== PAGE 170 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 167 
 
 
1.5.4. 
Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders 
53. 
As part of the project development, the AE carried out consultation workshops for key 
stakeholders including government agencies, financial institutions, representatives of textile 
and RMG companies, academia and civil society organizations. Reports of the consultation 
workshops are attached to the funding proposal. 
54. 
A no-objection letter issued by the Ministry of Finance, Government of Bangladesh, the 
GCF national designated authority, is attached to the funding proposal.  
55. 
The TAP considers the programme’s country ownership as medium to high. The 
programme is in line with the national policies and strategies that support energy efficiency in 
the industrial sector. It was developed in consultation with key stakeholders. The TAP considers 
that the AE has sufficient experience and capacity to implement the programme as presented. 
While the four LFIs do have exposure to the RMG sector, their capacity to implement the 
programme cannot be fully confirmed. 
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
             Scale: Medium 
1.6.1. 
Cost-effectiveness and efficiency regarding financial and non- financial aspects  
56. 
As per the AE’s projection, the programme intends to deliver total GHG emission savings 
of 14.5 MtCO2eq. Accordingly, the cost of GHG emission reductions per tCO2eq is estimated to be 
USD 23.4 against the total cost of funding of USD 340.5 million. With respect to GCF funding of 
USD 256.5 million, it is projected to be USD 23.4 per tCO2eq. 
57. 
The cost varies between the textile and RMG sectors: USD 34.3 per tCO2eq and USD 19.2 
per tCO2eq, respectively, against the programme funding. With respect to GCF funding, it is 
estimated to be USD 25.8 and USD 14.5 per tCO2eq for the textile and RMG sectors, respectively. 
1.6.2. 
Amount of co-financing  
58. 
The co-financing ratio of GCF financing is estimated to be 0.33. The programme requires 
a total financing of USD 340.5 million, of which USD 256.48 million is provided by GCF in the 
form of loan and grant. The balance of USD 84.02 million is to be provided by IDCOL, the four 
LFIs and SREDA in the form of a loan and in-kind contribution.  
59. 
The total amount of funding to be mobilized for purchasing energy-efficient equipment 
is expected to be higher than the total amount of the programme. IDCOL and four LFIs expect 
borrowers to provide equal amounts of funds to their loans. Accordingly, an additional USD 83.0 
million can be mobilized in the form of borrower’s equity contribution. If that amount is 
included, then the co-financing ratio goes up to 0.65.  
1.6.3. 
Programme/project financial viability and other financial indicators  
60. 
The programme promotes investments in energy efficiency by providing concessional 
financing so that the total cost of investment, including equipment, financial and operational 
costs that a company incurs for its purchase of energy-efficient equipment would not be 
disadvantageous when compared with that of conventional equipment. On the basis of a survey, 
the AE estimates that the upfront cost of energy-efficient equipment is approximately 25 per 
cent higher than that of conventional equipment. Therefore, the interest rate of the 
programme’s loans for the companies will be set lower than the market interest rate so that the 
present values of investment in energy-efficient equipment and conventional equipment 
become compatible. The loan tenor will extend up to 10 years to help companies manage yearly 
cash outflow.

=== PAGE 171 ===
GCF/B.27/02/Add.10/Rev.01 
Page 168 
 
 
1.6.4. 
Industry best practices  
61. 
The programme will help the textile and RMG sectors to showcase the financial and 
operational merits of energy-efficient equipment and technologies and establish energy-
efficient practices in Bangladesh. The three technical components include assistance to the EEs 
and private sector companies in the sectors to enhance monitoring and market awareness. A 
technology fair will be organized within three years of the start of implementation, to promote 
market awareness and access to energy-efficient technologies for both sectors. The government 
agencies will be supported so that challenges identified in the programme can be shared and 
lessons learned can be reflected in developing enabling policies and regulations for investments 
in energy efficiency in Bangladesh.  
62. 
The TAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the programme as medium. 
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
63. 
The TAP recommends that the Board consider the proposed programme with the 
following two conditions: 
(a) 
The AE shall monitor and report to GCF actual annual energy consumption of each piece 
of eligible energy-efficient equipment and technology which has been financed by the 
Programme, for as long as any amount of the related sub-loan is outstanding; and 
(b) 
The AE shall monitor and report to GCF annual energy consumption of each piece of 
equipment and technology that has been replaced as a result of the Programme, from 
the date it was replaced until: 
(i) 
The sixth anniversary of the replacement; or  
(ii) 
The relevant equipment or technology has been scrapped and retired from 
operation, whichever is earlier. 
64. 
The programme offers concessional funding to finance energy efficiency investments for 
replacement and expansion in the textile and RMG sectors in Bangladesh. The AE assumes that 
replaced equipment would be resold at the secondary market rate for replacement of even less 
efficient equipment. Accordingly, there would be no leakage with respect to emission 
reductions. However, the TAP is unable to fully agree to the AE’s assumption, because that 
would not be always the case. Rather, the likely case is that replaced equipment would be 
purchased on the secondary market for the purpose of expansion to catch up with market 
growth, rather than replacement, especially if textile and RMG factories have limited interest in 
energy efficiency.  
65. 
Further, the TAP sees a risk that the replaced equipment would be operated longer in 
total than its expected lifetime so the estimated impact on emission reductions could be larger 
than that assumed. In that case, the estimated emission reductions of the programme would 
turn out to be less. To validate and confirm the leakage of emission reductions, it is imperative 
that actual energy consumption of all equipment replaced is actually monitored so that GCF can 
reasonably evaluate the programme’s ultimate impact with respect to GHG emission reductions.  
66. 
Without the strong monitoring plan, the TAP would recommend that the Board consider 
a request to the AE to ensure all of the replaced equipment to be scrapped and retired from 
operation.

Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel  
assessment findings (FP150) 
Proposal name: 
Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of 
energy saving technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade 
Garment (RMG) sectors of Bangladesh 
Accredited entity: 
Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL) 
 
Impact potential 
As rightfully mentioned in the ITAP assessment that the the textile sector (spinning, weaving, knitting and 
dyeing), and readymade garment (RMG) sector (cutting, sewing, dyeing, ironing, and finishing) have 
substantial scope of energy savings. The RMG sector is the most energy intensive in the industry 
consuming 15.4 per cent of the total industrial energy demand in Bangladesh. The textile sector follows 
the RMG subsector with its demand representing 12.4 per cent of that of the total industry. The proposed 
programme ““Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving 
technologies and equipment for Textile and Readymade Garment (RMG) sectors of Bangladesh” aims to 
achieve the Fund’s objective and result areas by contributing shift to low-emission sustainable 
development pathways. The contribution is achieved by providing target market i.e. Textile and RMG 
sector of Bangladesh, access to concessional line of financing for undertaking energy efficiency measures 
which will result in 14.53 MtCO2 emissions savings as well as provide market participants with 
appropriate technical support required for stimulating the energy efficiency market in Bangladesh, as 
well as to support implementation of energy efficiency initiatives.   
 
Paradigm shift potential 
The paradigm shift potential is rightly described in the ITAP assessment, IDCOL as a government owned 
development financial institution will strongly support government’s target and vision to enable the 
industrial energy transition to low carbon energy system through major increase in energy efficiency. The 
success of the proposed programme will enable replication of similar programmes in other sectors of the 
country. 
 
Sustainable development potential 
IDCOL agrees with the remarks made by ITAP regarding the programme’s sustainable development 
potential. Delivering a large-scale retrofit initiative through this programme will have large and 
important development benefits whose impacts will increase over time as energy prices rise. 
Needs of the recipient 
 
Bangladesh is in dire need of concessional financing, capacity development and marketing activation to 
realize the potential of industrial energy efficiency. The need of the proposed programme is indeed high, 
especially considering the severe impact that the manufacturing sector has faced due to impacts of 
COVID-19.  
 
Country ownership

higher. The proposed programme is regarded in the Country Programme (A16 & A17) as of strategic

Executive Summary 
Ready-made garment (RMG) sector in Bangladesh plays a key role in the economic 
development of the country as one of the biggest industrial contributors to the country’s gross 
domestic product (GDP). The RMG industry now contributes over 83% to Bangladesh’s export 
income. Moreover, this sector is one of the major sources of employment for women in 
Bangladesh, especially for the poor and unprivileged women. Women were the main 
workforce in the country's apparel sector since the sector began its journey in 1970’s. The 
RMG sector has been a key enabler in overall socio-economic development of Bangladesh. 
In common perspective, a majority of the 4-5 million people employed in the sector are women 
- 80% of the workers and more than 60% of the total workforce for the sector are women.  
 
The employment of these women, mostly from rural areas, have provided them with financial 
freedom and greater social independence. Compared to other industries, women employment 
rate is higher in RMG sector in Bangladesh. So, for implementing any project in the RMG 
sector it is very important to ensure equal participation of both male and female in all the 
project interventions. The GCF also promotes environmental, social, economic and 
development co-benefits through taking a gender-sensitive approach. In this regard, this 
project aims to assess the gender dimensions in RMG sector in Bangladesh during project 
preparation, implementation and monitoring stage. For this purpose, the study team 
conducted stakeholder consultations and field surveys to collect information and analyzed 
those data through a gender lens. The results were incorporated throughout the other project 
documents and mainstreamed into the mitigation and adaptation initiatives to obtain in such a 
way that gender co-benefits are obtained. 
 
The objective of the Gender Assessment was to identify the gender aspects of the project 
activities, including selection of employees and their trainings, to collect information from 
various stakeholders such as workers, supervisors, managers, key informants (women and 
men) and to learn the impact of the project on empowerment of different categories of women 
and men. This tool also helped to promote gender – relevant entry points, policies and identify 
opportunities for enhancing gender equality in this project. 
 
Through this gender action plan the project aims to ensure that the services and technologies 
provided by the project/program be available and accessible to both women and men, equal 
access for the women and men from vulnerable communities to information and opportunities 
necessary to participate and benefit fully from the anticipated outcomes of the project and 
promote leadership capacity of women. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
174

=== PAGE 178 ===
5 
1 Introduction 
 
Improved energy efficiency in Bangladesh can lead to greater business growth and profitability 
at the local level; at the national level it supports GDP growth and enhances energy security, 
and at a global level it helps mitigate climate change and ensures sustainable global growth. 
For industries, energy efficiency reduces production costs and increases profits. This also 
reduces their carbon footprint at no additional cost (an important consideration in the face of 
international buyers' requirements). Among all the sub-sectors, Garment and textile sector 
(manufacturing sector) consumes maximum energy. As Garment and Textile sector consumes 
maximum energy, it also holds a significant potential to reduce energy consumption. Valuing 
these benefits, in the current environment in Bangladesh, if energy intensive industries 
(Garment and textile) in sectors with high savings potential start utilizing energy efficient 
technologies they will be poised to capture huge future economic opportunities. In addition to 
such economic benefits, the environmental impact of decreased energy usage and reduced 
carbon emissions, social impact by providing well-being and employment will create a more 
sustainable Bangladesh for future generations.  
 
Based on the existing scenario of RMG sector in Bangladesh, it is evident that adoption of 
energy efficiency measures can considerably improve socio-economic impact of the sector as 
well as reduce environmental impact. Infrastructure Development Company Limited (IDCOL) 
intends to realize these benefits by developing and implementing a program to support 
entrepreneurs and business establishments with financial and market resources to avail 
investment opportunities for energy saving technology upgrades. IDCOL has solicited financial 
support from Green Climate Fund (GCF) to execute this program. This fund will be then utilized 
as concessional loans to finance garment business via four selected banks i.e. City Bank Ltd, 
Southeast Bank Ltd, BRAC Bank Ltd and IDLC Finance Ltd.  
 
Ready-made garment (RMG) sector in Bangladesh plays a key role in the economic 
development of the country as one of the biggest industrial contributors to the country’s gross 
domestic product (GDP). The RMG industry now contributes over 83% to Bangladesh’s export 
income.1 Moreover, this sector is one of the major sources of employment for women in 
Bangladesh, especially for the poor and unprivileged women. Women were the main 
workforce in the country's apparel sector since the sector began its journey in 1970’s. The 
RMG sector has been a key enabler in overall socio-economic development of Bangladesh. 
In common perspective, a majority of the 4-5 million people employed in the sector are women 
- 80% of the workers and more than 60% of the total workforce for the sector are women.  
 
The employment of these women, mostly from rural areas, have provided them with financial 
freedom and greater social independence. Compared to other industries, women employment 
rate is higher in RMG sector in Bangladesh. However, the percentage of female workers have 
declined in the recent years. According to a survey of Center for Policy Dialogue (CPD), 
released on March 2018, among garment worker 60.8% are female & 39.20% are male. The 
survey was conducted on 2270 workers in 193 factories in Dhaka, Gazipur, Narayanganj & 
Chottogram. This situation has been created in the sector due to increasing use of technology, 
 
1

lack of skills of women workers compared to males, increasing interest of men for salary rise, 
social inequalities and due to labor-intensive nature of the sector. Even, the average age of 
women working in this sector is lower than that of men. In addition, there is discrimination in 
areas of service book, working hour including overtime, leaves (casual, sick, annual), 
separation of rest room, social protection like provident fund and group insurance, and 
representational rights.  
 
After the Rana Plaza incident, more emphasis has been given on the compliance issues in 
the RMG sector. All the owners are obliged to ensure health and safety issues for the workers 
and ensure equal facilities for both genders. International buyers also give importance on 
compliance issues while placing order and usually select those that are more complaint. 
However, due to lack of financial support, many small and medium enterprises often lag 
behind to ensure the required facilities for women, especially pregnant women and mothers 
with dependent children. So, with this programme there will be an opportunity for the RMG 
units to include better facilities for women and address compliance issues as well. This way 
they will be able to attract more international buyers and access better opportunities for future 
development. 
 
As such, gender equity is crucial to the sustainable development of all sectors of the economy. 
GCF also gives special attention on ensuring gender equity and focuses on gender-responsive 
climate action programmes and projects that benefit women and men. The GCF Governing 
Instrument states that: “The Fund will strive to maximize the impact of its funding for adaptation 
and mitigation … promoting environmental, social, economic and development co-benefits 
and taking a gender-sensitive approach.” In this regard, this programme aims to assess 
gender dimensions in RMG sector in Bangladesh during project preparation, implementation 
and monitoring stage. For this purpose, the study team conducted stakeholder consultations 
and field surveys to collect information and analyzed those data. The results were 
incorporated throughout the other project documents and mainstreamed into the mitigation 
and adaptation initiatives to obtain in such a way that gender co-benefits are obtained. 
 
2 Rational of Gender Assessment 
The Gender Assessment will help to understand relationship between men and women, their 
access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative to each other. A 
comprehensive gender assessment entails the examination of the different roles, rights, 
needs, and opportunities of women and men, boys and girls in this project context. It is a tool 
that will help to promote gender – relevant entry points, policies and identify opportunities for 
enhancing gender equality in this project. GCF also puts emphasis on the ensuring gender 
equity in all project level activities. In this regard, a Gender Assessment was conducted for 
mainstreaming gender into the project level activities. The objective of the Gender 
Assessment was- 
• 
to identify the gender aspects of the project activities, including selection of employees 
and their trainings. 
• 
to collect information from various stakeholders such as workers, supervisors, 
managers, key informants (women and men); 
• 
to learn the impact of the project on empowerment of different categories of women 
and men; 
176

=== PAGE 180 ===
7 
3 Methodology of the Gender Assessment 
 
The assessment studied the engagement of men and women in the RMG sector in 
Bangladesh, labor force participation, access and control to technology and financial 
resources, as well as power relations. The study team gathered information through literature 
review, surveys and stakeholder analysis. Also, household surveys were conducted to get the 
perspective of the community people living in the vicinity of the factory. Detailed methodology 
is described below: 
 
3.1 Literature Review 
At the inception phase of the project the team performed literature review from secondary 
sources to understand the baseline situation of the RMG sector in Bangladesh to understand 
the baseline situation of RMG sector and the broad scope for EE applications in the sector 
considering gender dimensions. In this regard, the project team analyzed relevant reports, 
about national policies and guidelines (environmental, social, gender equality), potential risks 
identified in the RMG sector, historical studies and research on Energy Efficiency (EE) in 
Bangladesh’s RMG sector, international best practices, potential EE interventions etc. 
considering gender equity.  
 
3.2 Factory Survey and Household Survey 
The study team conducted open inquiries of facility managers and workers of RMG factories 
and community people living near the selected RMG units on energy efficiency, environment 
and social issues related to RMG sector focusing on gender. For this purpose, 80 respondents 
from 20 RMG units and 150 households were selected in four key garment hubs i.e. in Dhaka, 
Gazipur, Narayanganj and Chattogram district. In each of the RMG units, key informant 
interviews (KII) were conducted with facility managers and workers and field group 
discussions (FGD) were conducted with a group of 10-15 people including workers and facility 
managers. While choosing the respondents for FGD, the study team tried to maintain gender 
balance by including both male and female representatives. However, during the survey 
period, no female facility manager was identified so there is no representation of women in 
the information shared by facility managers.  
 
3.3 Key Informant Interview with Experts 
Key informant interviews (KIIs) have been conducted to supplement the information mined 
from secondary sources as well as to enhance the understanding of the ground realities of the 
RMG sector. For KII representatives were selected from government ministries, NGO, INGO, 
development partners, academicians, financial institution, monitoring agencies for RMG 
sector etc. A total of 25 KIIs were conducted based on a questionnaire which contain queries 
related to the following thematic areas: 
• 
Growth prospects of RMG sector 
• 
Compliance with environmental, social and gender (ESG) related requirements 
• 
Energy efficiency adoption in sector 
• 
Financing energy efficiency initiatives.  
 
177

=== PAGE 181 ===
8 
During the KIIs the study team tried to maintain a gender balance by including both male and 
female representatives from each sector. But in most of the cases it was not possible due to 
absence of female leadership in the sector. However, at least one woman was interviewed 
from selected stakeholder categories i.e., from selected government ministries, NGO, INGO, 
development partners, academicians, financial institution, monitoring agencies (BGMEA, 
BKMEA) working for RMG sector etc. 
  
3.4 Stakeholder Engagement Workshop 
While preparing the proposal, stakeholder engagement was a prerequisite. In this regard, the 
information gathered during the literature review and surveys were presented during the 
stakeholder workshops where stakeholders and beneficiaries discussed on existing issues 
and concerns pertaining to Environment, Society and Gender in the Garment sector. A wide 
range of key stakeholders i.e. energy efficiency practitioners including regulators, policy 
makers, experts and garment sector entrepreneurs, as well as environment, social and gender 
experts actively participated and shared their insights and experiences. The workshop clarified 
the framework of the proposed GCF proposal along with the key indicators with the 
stakeholders and participants. The outcome of the workshops was noted and relevant 
changes in the project documents were made accordingly. The detail findings from the 
workshops have been presented in a separate document of workshop report. 
 
Both the workshops were well attended. A wide range of energy efficiency practitioners 
including regulators, policy makers, experts, academicians, researchers, garment sector 
entrepreneurs, NGO, INGO, development organizations, CSOs as well as environment, social 
and gender experts were attended the workshop. In the first workshop total 46 participants 
were present among them 15% were female participants and in the second workshop there 
were 45 participants where 11% were female participants. Even though RMG sector occupies 
a high number of women workers, there few women involved in the higher management. Also, 
very few organizations have women involved in project management or research regarding 
RMG sector related issues. So, the representation of women was less compared to men in 
the workshops. However, in both the workshops it was maintained that at least one woman 
was present from relevant government ministries and departments, monitoring agencies 
(BGMEA, BKMEA), academicians, researchers, garment sector entrepreneurs, NGO, INGO, 
development organizations, CSOs and sectoral experts.

9 
4 Policies and Regulations 
4.1 National Policy, Strategy and Framework 
The government of Bangladesh recognizes the need for coordinated efforts on gender issues 
and the need to mainstream gender issues in development initiatives to increase resilience. 
At present Bangladesh is engaged in aligning national legislation and policies with its 
international commitments to strengthen gender dimensions. Some key policies are with 
regards to climate change and adaptation and the provision of gender issues in those policies 
are highlighted below. In addition, there is also a set of supporting ‘Rules’ and ‘Regulations’ 
that include important provisions. 
 
The Constitution of Bangladesh has recognized fundamental rights of women workers at 
work places including necessary social protection (Articles, 28, 29, 34, 38, 14, 15 and 20(1), 
(The Constitution of Bangladesh). Bangladesh Constitution provides that the state shall not 
discriminate against any citizen on the grounds of religion, race, caste or place of birth and 
women shall have equal rights with men in all spheres of the state or public life (Article 28). 
Bangladesh as a state is responsible for its citizens to ensure the right to work that is the right 
to guaranteed employment at a reasonable wage having regard to the quantity and quality of 
works, and reasonable rest, recreation and leisure (Article 15). Bangladesh Constitution 
recognizes that everyone shall be paid for work on the basis of the principle from each 
according to his abilities (Article 20). In addition, the fundamental rights guaranteed in Chapter 
III of Bangladesh Constitution, especially relevant to workers' rights, prohibits all forms of 
forced labour and makes it a punishable offence (Article 34) and guarantees the right to 
freedom of association and to form trade unions (Article 38). 
 
Bangladesh Labour Act, 2006 (BLA) has included a large number of labour rights for 
protection of women workers. BLA has various positive points relating to labour rights, but due 
to its various limitations, absence of adequate penalty in statutory law for employers and lack 
of proper code of conduct by the garment authorities, there is scope of violation of labor rights 
in some areas.  
 
The BLA 2006, mainly focus on the following major components: 
a. Sanitation and Hygiene: From section 51-59, BLA 2006, the terms and conditions of 
cleanliness, ventilation and temperature, dust and fume, disposal of waste and effluents, 
overcrowding, lighting, drinking water, latrines and urinals, were discussed. In section 59, 
it is clearly mentioned to provide distinct toilet facility for men and women along with proper 
cleaning maintenance. 
b. Maternity Benefit: Every pregnant women worker can get the maternity benefit of eight 
weeks, preceding the expected day of her delivery and eight weeks immediately following 
the day of her delivery (Section 45-46). The procedure and amount of regarding payment 
of maternity benefit are also mentioned in Section 47- 49. 
c. Working conditions and hours: Employment of women, adolescents or children in 
specific works (such as the dangerous operations, underground or underwater work) is 
prohibited or restricted (Section 39, 40, 42 and 79). Furthermore, without the consent of 
women worker, working between 10.00PM to 06.00AM is not allowed (Section 109). 
179

=== PAGE 183 ===
10 
d. Safety: According to Section 89, At least one first-aid box for every one hundred fifty 
workers must be accessible and maintained. Also, for the establishments with forty or more 
workers, a child care room with proper facilities should be provided (Section 94). 
e. Trade Union: If 20% of the total workers or members are women than while establishing 
trade union, the executive body must comprise minimum 10% women members (Section 
176e, BLA, 2013). 
f. Indecent behavior: Despite of the rank or status of a woman worker, no one of the 
establishment can treat or behave in indecent or improper manner with her (Section 332, 
BLA, 2013). 
g. Institutional framework: If factories occupies 50 or more workers than safety committees 
must be formed (Section 90A, BLA, 2013), a Safety welfare officers shall be appointed 
and the cost of treatment of occupational diseases will be covered (Section 150(3), BLA, 
2013) if the establishment holds more than 500 workers (Section 89(8), BLA, 2013) and 
workplaces with more than 5000 members or staffs should establish workplace health 
centres (Section 89(6), BLA, 2013). 
 
Bangladesh Labor Rules, 2015 emphasizes on gender equity and has special provision for 
pregnant women. According to the Bangladesh Labor Rules, 2015, the owner and other 
workers should have the following responsibilities to a pregnant worker, such as: 
(a) Such behavior or comment should not be made so that she gets insulted or humiliated 
(b) Not engage her in hazardous work declared by the government or any work hazardous for 
her health. 
(c) To transfer or post her to any work where there is no hazard. 
(d) To give priority to use the lift during the work. 
(e) After the delivery, to make opportunity for breastfeeding and ensure the congenial 
atmosphere for this. 
 
Right to Occupational Safety and Health BLA, 2006 prescribes provisions relating to 
occupational hygiene, occupational diseases, safety measures, industrial accidents, 
protection of women and young persons in dangerous occupations and also covers conditions 
of work, working hours, welfare facilities, holidays, leave etc. (Sections 11, 51, 52, 53, 56, 57, 
58, 61, 62, 91, 92, 93,94 and, 109, BLA, 2006). Most of the provisions lack in standard values 
and are not specific rather general in nature. In terms of occupational safety and health issues, 
the status of Bangladesh is very poor and women workers are extremely vulnerable in this 
regard. 
 
Section 195 of the Bangladesh Labor Act, 2006 (amended 2013) outlaws numerous “unfair 
labor practices.” For example, no employer shall “dismiss, discharge, remove from 
employment, or threaten to dismiss, discharge, or remove from employment a worker, or injure 
or threaten to injure him in respect of his employment by reason that the worker is or proposes 
to become, or seeks to persuade any other person to become, a member or officer of a trade 
union.” 
 
The National Women Development Policy (2011) briefly promotes women’s role in 
environmental management and the importance of ensuring facilities for and the security of 
pregnant women in the event of natural disasters. It emphasizes on elimination of all forms of 
violence to women including physical and mental abuse and sexual harassment, rape, dowry, 
family abuse and acid throwing in family, society and in the workplace. The policy ensures 
180

=== PAGE 184 ===
11 
participation of women in decision making regarding environment management and pollution 
control and program implementation. It also ensures gender equal rate of wages, increase 
participation of women in the labor market, equal opportunity at the workplace, ensured 
security and removal of disparities in employment.  
 
The policy aims to motivate all employment agencies to give women all kinds of equal 
opportunities under government quota and employment policy and create necessary 
environment to promote access of women to employment arras at increasing rate, their 
position as such and continue their advancement. In addition, it promotes to make necessary 
reforms of all concerned law, rule and policy for wider employment of women. In recognition 
of the contribution of women in the management of natural resources conservation and a safer 
environment to give them opportunity of equal participation in environment preservation policy 
and programs reflecting a women perspective. Also, the policy emphasizes to undertake all 
out initiative for employment of the educated and illiterate women labor.  
 
The 7th Five Year Plan (2016-2020) has also highlighted the aspects for worker safety.  It 
was mentioned in the 7FYP that higher growth in Bangladesh was benefitted from a rising 
labour force both due to the demographic transition that produced a larger share of working 
population and an increasing labour force participation rate of the female labour. But wage 
rates tend to be lower for female workers relative to male and proper employment regulations 
to facilitate the participation of female workers (e.g. maternity leave, skill development, child 
care facilities, etc.) have to be put in place in most areas of employment. The RMG industry 
has shown the way. However, safety standards in RMG are becoming a major challenge for 
sustaining the continued expansion of RMG sector. Thanks to recent coordinated efforts of 
the Government with the Apparel Manufacturers, and the buyer-driven initiatives. Substantial 
progresses in ensuring worker safety standards and improvement in working conditions have 
been achieved. 
 
Bangladesh has ratified International Labor Organization (ILO) conventions 87 and 98 
on freedom of association and collective bargaining and is required to protect the rights 
contained in them. At the international level, Bangladesh has also ratified the UN Convention 
on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), as well as 
different ILO Convention. 
 
Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan 2009 (BCCSAP) has a programme 
for strengthening gender consideration for climate change management. Under this 
programme it was mentioned that gender consideration is needing to be included in all climate 
responsive activities.   
 
Bangladesh Climate Change and Gender Action Plan (ccGAP: Bangladesh) 2013 has 
been prepared with an aim to ensure gender equality in climate change-related policies, 
strategies and interventions. This policy has highlighted the importance of equal participation 
of women and men in climate change mitigation practices and low carbon development. Some 
of the major points that are addressed in the action plan are following: 
a. Ensure that gender considerations are addressed in the process of reviewing energy and 
technology policies. 
b. Develop gender responsive programmemes to reduce GHG emission at household (HH) 
while ensuring women’s access to energy and power technologies. 
181

=== PAGE 185 ===
12 
c. Improve women’s knowledge on climate change mitigation issues. 
d. Develop women’s entrepreneurship related to waste management. 
e. Increase women’s participation in solid waste and drainage management. 
f. Mainstream gender considerations in coastal and social forestry programmemes or 
initiatives. 
 
In addition, the ccGAP: Bangladesh 2013 has also put emphasis on enhancing knowledge 
and access to financial instruments for women. 
 
The primary responsibility for protecting the rights of workers rests with the Bangladesh 
government. However, there are some professional bodies such as Bangladesh Garment 
Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA), and the Bangladesh Knitwear 
Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BKMEA) who can do to ensure compliance with 
such provisions, and to sanction companies that abuse worker rights. However, factory 
owners can carry considerable political clout in Bangladesh and this can act as a barrier to 
holding them to account for violating workplace rights, as well as health and safety provisions. 
 
The United Nations Collaborative Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and 
Forest Degradation in Developing Countries + (REDD+) Readiness Roadmap (2012) pays 
attention to gender by taking initiative to identify gender-specific risks of the REDD+ 
Readiness development process in Bangladesh. In addition, the roadmap describes activities 
and institutional development that ensure ‘gender-neutral outcomes.’ 
 
International companies that purchase clothes and other products from Bangladesh factories 
also have a responsibility to ensure that worker safety and rights are maintained throughout 
their supply chains. Many factory conditions described in this report not only violate 
Bangladesh’s labor law, but also breach the standards that Western high street retailers insist 
that their suppliers follow.  
 
4.2 International Policy and Framework 
4.2.1 Green Climate Fund (GCF) 
The GCF provides clear definitions and differences between key gender terms; Gender, 
Gender Equality, Gender Equity and Gender Sensitivity. 
 
In accordance with the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan, the core elements of gender 
mainstreaming required in GCF project design include: 
• 
Country ownership in terms of alignment with national policies and priorities; 
• 
A mandatory initial socioeconomic and gender assessment, to proactively build in a 
gender-sensitive approach to project planning design and implementation 
arrangements; 
• 
Gender equitable stakeholder consultations based on the gender parameters in the 
policy; 
• 
Determination of how the project/programme can respond to the needs of women and 
men in view of the specific climate change issue; 
• 
Identification of drivers of change and gender dynamics required to achieve the 
project/programme adaptation or mitigation goals;  
182

=== PAGE 186 ===
13 
• 
Identification and design of specific gender elements to be included in 
project/programme activities. 
 
Mainstreaming gender in GCF projects can be synthesized into a three-pronged approach on 
a gendered project cycle: 
• 
Gender analysis: to understand the social, economic and political factors underlying 
climate change-exacerbated gender inequality, and the potential contributions of 
women and men to societal changes in order to build resilience to and address climate 
change; 
• 
Gendered actions: methods and tools to promote gender equality and reduce gender 
disparities in climate action; and 
• 
Gender-sensitive M&E: measuring the outcomes and impacts of project activities on 
women and men’s resilience to climate change through gender responsive M&E. 
 
4.2.2 World Bank Group 
The World Bank recognizes in its Gender and Development Policy Framework that gender 
issues are important dimensions of its poverty reduction, economic growth, human well-being 
and development effectiveness agenda. According to the gender mainstreaming strategy, 
“The term gender refers to culturally based expectations of the roles and behaviors of males 
and females. The term distinguishes the socially constructed from the biologically determined 
aspects of being male and female.” 
 
The Bank Operational Policy Focused Specifically on Gender and Development (OP/BP 4.20) 
has been developed with the following objectives: 
• 
Providing a country-level, strategic Approach to mainstreaming gender issues 
• 
Country-level Gender Assessments 
• 
Integration of gender assessment findings in the Country Assistance Strategy (CAS) 
developed by the bank for each donor country. It draws on and discusses the findings 
of the Gender Assessment and links policy provisions for gender (OP/BP 4.20) and 
policy provisions for CAS preparation and consultation (BP 2.11) 
• 
Integration of gender issues into operations of interventions established under the 
CAS. 
 
Based on the policy guidelines, a Gender Assessment (whether stand alone, as part of a 
broader ESW, or done outside the Bank) should typically include the following aspects: 
a. A brief description of: 
i. 
The different socioeconomic roles of females and males, including their 
participation in both the market and household economies; 
ii. 
male-female disparities in access to, control over, and use of assets and productive 
resources; 
iii. 
male-female disparities in human development indicators; 
iv. 
The relative participation of females and males in development decision-making; 
and 
v. 
Laws, institutional frameworks, norms, and other societal practices that lead 
(implicitly or explicitly) to gender discrimination and/or gender inequality; 
183

=== PAGE 187 ===
14 
b. A review of the country context, including the country’s policies, priorities, legal and 
regulatory framework, and institutional arrangements for implementing its gender and 
development goals; and 
c. Based on (a) and (b) above, suggested gender-responsive policies and interventions 
that are relevant for reducing poverty and enhancing economic growth, human well-
being, and development effectiveness. 
 
4.2.3 Asian Development Bank (ADB) 
ADB’s policy on Gender and Development (GAD) adopts mainstreaming as a key strategy in 
promoting gender equity. The key elements of ADB’s policy include the following. 
a. Gender sensitivity: to observe how ADB operations affect women and men, and to 
consider women’s needs and perspectives in planning its operations. 
b. Gender analysis: to assess systematically the impact of a project on men and women, 
and on the economic and social relationship between them. 
c. Gender planning: to formulate specific strategies that aim to bring about equal 
opportunities for men and women. 
d. Mainstreaming: to consider gender issues in all aspects of ADB operations, 
accompanied by efforts to encourage women’s participation in the decision-making 
process in development activities. 
e. Agenda setting: to assist DMC governments in formulating strategies to reduce gender 
disparities and in developing plans and targets for women’s and girls’ education, 
health, legal rights, employment, and income-earning opportunities. 
 
In 2013, ADB adopted a new Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Operational Plan 
2013-2020 that sets out the strategic directions and guiding framework for moving the gender 
equity agenda forward, and for better aligning gender operations with Strategy 2020. 
 
The outcome of the gender equality operational plan is improved gender equality results of 
ADB operations by 2020. While gender mainstreaming will remain the primary and priority 
approach, on its own it may not be sufficient to narrow persisting gender gaps and inequalities. 
Targeted public policies and priority investments in women and girls are required to accelerate 
closing remaining gender gaps and achieving better gender equality outcomes. 
Opportunities for direct investment in women and girls to narrow gender disparities will be 
explored in areas such as: 
a. 
girl’s secondary education and completion; 
b. 
access to productive assets, labor saving technology, jobs, and income earning 
opportunities; 
c. 
school-to-work transition programs and skills training for employment, especially 
for young women; 
d. 
business development services for women entrepreneurs; (v) financial services 
and access to credit; 
e. 
policy and legal reforms to tackle issues of gender-based violence and anti-
trafficking 
f. 
giving women voice in decision-making in both formal and informal institutions. 
 
184

=== PAGE 188 ===
15 
These complimentary and more targeted initiatives are more likely to deliver gender equality 
outcomes faster and to equip women to better capture and benefit from the opportunities 
provided through gender mainstreaming. 
 
4.2.4 International Finance Corporation (IFC) 
Supporting gender equality is a strategic priority for IFC, which provides advice and 
investments in partner countries and companies that help expand and strengthen the role of 
women.  IFC's strategic priorities for Gender: 
1. Closing gaps in health and education 
2. Supporting more and better job opportunities 
3. Helping increase access to finance, insurance, technology, and land 
4. Enhancing women’s leadership roles 
 
IFC works to reduce gaps between men and women in the private sector, enabling companies 
and economies to improve their performance. Too often, women's private-sector participation 
is under-valued. Integrating women better into business operations can increase companies’ 
profitability and contribute significantly to shared prosperity. However, women have less 
access to equal employment opportunities and capital to grow their businesses, experience 
high levels of sexual violence, are often barred from owning or inheriting land or other property, 
and have less income to invest for the future. 
 
IFC and its partners are addressing these challenges by working with clients to ensure that 
women and men can equally participate in creating business value and secure influence and 
income for themselves. When men and women alike are allowed to make decisions, pursue 
employment, entrepreneurship, and leadership, societies can reach their economic potential. 
 
In the IFC Performance Standards (PS 1-8), gender inclusion is addressed as a cross-cutting 
issue across multiple Performance Standards especially for assessment and management of 
environmental and social risks and impacts (PS-1) and labor and working conditions (PS-2).  
 
Further, IFC has developed a toolkit for addressing the specific gender related issues. The 
toolkit is a modular, customizable guide that includes four tool suites focused on different 
issues: 
a. TOOL SUITE 1: Increasing Gender Diversity from the Workforce to the Boardroom 
Tool Suite 1 presents a detailed business case for increasing gender balance on 
boards, in senior management, and in the workforce. It features 16 tools to help 
companies:  
i. 
Assess their gender-balance performance at all levels of their organization 
ii. 
Increase gender balance in their workforce  
iii. 
Monitor and evaluate progress towards gender diversity targets 
b. TOOL SUITE 2: Women-Owned Businesses and the Supply Chain 
Tool Suite 2 presents a detailed business case for increasing the gender diversity of 
businesses that are part of an OGM company’s supply chain. It features six tools to 
help companies: 
i. 
Evaluate their current supply chain  
ii. 
Develop gender-sensitive criteria for qualified vendors to improve outreach to 
and development of women-owned businesses  
185

=== PAGE 189 ===
16 
iii. 
Monitor and sustain progress towards a more gender-diverse supply chain 
c. TOOL SUITE 3: Women and Community Engagement 
Tool Suite 3 presents the business case for considering the issues, perspectives, and 
concerns of female community members in a company’s community engagement 
activities. It features 10 tools to help companies: 
i. 
Assess the degree of gender sensitivity in their current community engagement 
approaches  
ii. 
Integrate gender into existing community engagement assessments and 
activities  
iii. 
Monitor and sustain progress towards a more gender-equitable community 
engagement strategy and activity portfolio 
d. TOOL SUITE 4: Addressing Gender-Based Violence (GBV) in the Workforce 
Tool Suite 4 addresses and defines GBV, including intimate partner violence (IPV), 
sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA), and sexual harassment. It features seven tools 
to help companies:  
i. 
Recognize, address, and support victims of workplace sexual harassment  
ii. 
Support employees who may have experienced IPV and SEA in the community  
iii. 
Design activities as part of their community engagement portfolio, to recognize 
and address forms of GBV occurring in the community 
iv. 
Monitor and sustain progress towards reducing GBV 
 
4.2.5 Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA) 
JICA has three Strategic Development Objectives (SDOs) for “gender and development,” with 
effective approaches, trends, and possible future directions. These three objectives are 
introduced below. The first and the second objectives correspond to the “principal objectives” 
in DAC classification of aid contribution, while the third corresponds to the “significant 
objective” of the same.  
 
JICA envisions “Inclusive and Dynamic Development” in its efforts for promoting human and 
to provide timely, comprehensive support to those in developing countries. JICA recognizes 
women as a key agent for change in development and ensures that women have an equal 
voice and leadership in decision-making in all its interventions. JICA will also enhance 
women’s participation in leadership roles in all stages of natural disasters from DRR, to 
emergency relief and to reconstruction. It will also facilitate the formation of policies from a 
gender and diverse group perspective. JICA’s work will be responsive to the different needs 
and priorities of women and diverse groups as well as their security as it actively provides 
support in building societies full of hope and dignity. 
 
Priority Areas: 
i. 
Women’s Economic Empowerment; Promote women’s Livelihood, employment, 
entrepreneurship and business development 
ii. 
Women’s Rights and Security; Ensure women’s rights and security in conflicts, 
natural disasters and other crisis including Trafficking in persons and Gender 
based violence 
iii. 
Women’s Education and lifetime Health; Promote women’s lifetime health and 
education to enhance their autonomy
