4.3 Gender Related Policies of IDCOL and Local Financial 
Institutions (LFIs) 
4.3.1 IDCOL 
IDCOL makes the gender equity and equality a priority to maintain a safe, secured, positive, 
and collaborative culture in its work environment. IDCOL’s ESMF provides detail directions on 
integrating gender issues in development and assessing projects/programs which are 
following: 
 
a) IDCOL will assess internal mechanism and capacities to promote greater visibility of 
gender and social inclusion considerations in the design of its projects and programs.  
b) Ensure that all recipient projects and borrowers under the projects will identify and 
maximize opportunities for women’s participation in all employment opportunities 
during construction, operation and maintenance of infrastructure, with potential 
adoption of evidence-based gender related targets. 
c) Promote adherence to national core labor standards and commitments to gender 
equity and women’s empowerment stated in the legislations and regulations of the 
Government of Bangladesh.  
d) AssessthesocialandgenderimpactsoftheSolarHomeSystemsprogramandthe potential 
use of other identified women friendly technologies for greater social gender related 
benefits. 
e) Ensure the use of the Gender Action Framework. 
 
This Policy will guide in promoting gender equality, equity and awareness in its internal policies 
and procedures.  
 
Additionally, IDCOL has developed a Gender Policy for its organizational purpose. IDCOL 
believes that all staff members share a common interest in achieving the objectives of the 
organization and collaborative effort of the employees will ensure creating an environment 
that would promote teamwork and mutual respect. This Policy has been formulated to institute 
a systematic approach within the organizational practices in promoting gender sensitiveness, 
awareness, equity and equality in its internal environment and activities. Therefore, the 
Gender Policy along with ESMF will lead to implement gender sensitive approaches at IDCOL, 
its activities, and adherence to all other relevant laws, guidelines and policies. 
 
4.3.2 Local Financial Institutions (LFIs) 
There will be four local financial institutions (LFIs) involved in the programme who will channel 
the fund as concessional loans to finance garment business. These financial institutions are- 
City Bank Ltd, Southeast Bank Ltd, BRAC Bank Ltd and IDLC Finance Ltd. All of these 
institutions are well aware of the gender related concerns as they have experience of working 
187

=== PAGE 191 ===
18 
with readymade garments (RMG) sector. RMG sector in Bangladesh is one of those sectors 
that follows rules and regulations regarding gender quality especially in wage distribution, 
labor rights and occupational health and safety. Also, the current monitoring system in the 
RMG sector is well maintained and there is pressure from the international buyers as well. As 
a result, the LFIs are well aware of the gender related issues and its monitoring. A brief 
description of the existing policy and experience of the LFIs are following: 
 
IDLC Finance Limited follows the ten Principles of the United Nations Global Compact 
(UNGC) which are derived from Universal Declaration of Human Rights, International Labor 
Organization’s Declaration on Fundamental Principles and Rights at Work, Rio Declaration on 
Environment and Development, United Nations Convention Against Corruption. These 
Principles covers Human Rights, Labor, Environment and Anti-Corruption, and IDLC takes 
consent from respective clients for the same to approve loan applications. Thus, IDLC will 
consider gender issues to finance Energy Efficient projects under GCF by taking consent from 
clients considering wage level, overtime, leave policy, workplace health and safety (i.e., 
protective gears, fire safety, first-aid), freedom of association and/or collective bargaining 
provisions, and child labor.  
 
BRAC Bank has always been very much aligned with the gender equality, women 
empowerment and promoting leadership capacity of women when it comes to adhering ESG 
requirements within its organizational framework. On the policy level, they have their own 
Environmental & Social Safeguard Policy and Speak-up Policy where gender issues are 
highlighted with utmost priority.  
 
They have agreed to follow the following process under the guidance of Bank's Sustainable 
Finance Committee and on the working level, Sustainable Finance Unit will carry forward 
following activities to adhere GCF's ESG requirements: 
1) Review the existing Environmental & Social Safeguard Policy and add the new ESG 
requirements of GCF as part of it and get it approved by the management/board. 
2) Rollout capacity building sessions for business and risk assessment officials on the 
new ESG guidance note as prescribed by GCF. 
3) Follow GCF's prescribed methodology as well as already operating ESG risk 
assessment framework prior financing any under GCF. 
4) Yearly/half yearly monitoring of Bank as well as its client’s performance in dealing with 
ESG issues. 
 
Apart from Sustainable Finance Unit, BRAC Bank has their own Credit Inspection Team to 
monitor client's performance all over the year, hence conducting half-yearly or annual 
inspection will be a regular activity for us. 
 
Southeast Bank Limited (SEBL) does not have any specific gender related policy but they 
conduct periodic inspections at the project sites. Periodic and surprise inspections are among 
the keys risk management tools of SEBL. Most of the RMG clients of SEBL work with 
renowned international buyers who also have certain conditions precedent on gender related 
matters. For the proposed sub-projects, SEBL will conduct onsite as well as offsite periodic 
inspection for ensuring proper practice of gender related issues in the project. Also, SEBL will 
assess the client’s willingness and ability to implement gender issues as required by GCF and 
other regulatory authorities in the appraisal of loan proposal.  
188

=== PAGE 192 ===
19 
 
City Bank does not have any separate gender related policy for external project monitoring. 
However, they have an ESMS that is prepared following IFC Performance Standards and 
consider gender inclusion into the projects. Their customers have to fill up a declaration form 
before getting loan above a certain amount. This declaration form also has some criteria 
regarding gender issues. Also, they have a gender policy within their institutional framework 
to ensure gender equality in their organization and staff.  
 
City Bank conducts period audits and monitoring at project level and are positive about 
conducting half-yearly/ yearly monitoring after providing loans to the RMG units. During the 
monitoring procedure they will follow the gender action plan developed for this programme 
along with national policies and GCF guidelines for ensuring gender equality and women 
empowerment.

5 Findings from Gender Assessment  
5.1 Introduction 
Since its journey in Bangladesh, the RMG sector has created opportunities for people, 
especially for the poor and disadvantaged women to lead a dignified life. The industry plays a 
key role in employment generation and in the provision of income to the poor – it employs 
nearly 4.4 million workers2. The sector has also played a significant role in the socio-economic 
development of the country with women comprising nearly 80% of the total workforce in the 
sector3. It also plays a pivotal role to promote the development of linkage small scale 
industries, since 99% of the accessories like dyeing, printing, zippers, labels are manufactured 
in Bangladesh4. This had helped open employment opportunities through indirect economic 
activities, thereby contributing to the country’s overall socio-economic development, woman 
empowerment and poverty alleviation. 
 
It has seen tremendous growth and dominated the industrial sector of the country. Currently, 
there are more than 4,000 RMG firms (The Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and 
Exporters Association, 2019) in Bangladesh. Majority of those firms are locally owned with the 
exception of a few foreign firms located in export processing zones. Nearly 98% of RMG firms 
of Bangladesh are located mainly in 4 districts viz Dhaka (38.0%), Gazipur (28.9%), 
Chittagong (16.1%) and Narayanganj (14.7%)5.  Most of the factories are in different industrial 
clusters in these four districts due to availability of   better infrastructural facilities, access to 
major trade routes for import and export, logistic and banking facilities as well as easier 
availability of workers. Even within these regions there is a spread of RMG units within and 
beyond Export Processing Zones (EPZs) – only 1.5% of the total RMG enterprises are located 
in EPZs. This is due to the change in government legislation in 2010, allowing foreign direct 
investment (FDI) outside EPZs as well. Another key characteristic of the RMG sector in 
Bangladesh is that almost half of the units i.e. 48.9% are small enterprises which hire less 
than 500 workers6.  
 
However, women working in RMG sector are plagued with various problems, some of these 
are insufficient wage, irregularity in timeliness of wage payments and unsafe working 
conditions. Specifically, women workers have many issues that need to be taken care of 
especially in areas like workplace discrimination and career prospect, harassment, work and 
working time including rest, occupation health and safety, welfare provision, social protection 
and freedom of association, collective bargaining and social dialogue. Although gender-based 
wage discrimination is not seen that much now a days, discrimination exists in areas of equal 
treatment— women are forced to do overtime more than the men and the cut from the overtime 
allowance is more for women workers than men. Women workers face severe discrimination 
with regard to the scope of promotion and their career prospect is limited. A study shows that 
only 5-10% of the women workers eventually become supervisors (The Daily Star, March 8, 
 
2 Source: Information disseminated by BGMEA 
3 Source: Statement by BGMEA President (BGMEA website, 2019) 
4 Source: As per presentation provided by BGMEA representative  
5 Source: New Dynamics in Bangladesh’s Apparels Enterprises (CPD website, 2018) 
6 Source: New Dynamics in Bangladesh’s Apparels Enterprises (CPD website, 2018) 
190

=== PAGE 194 ===
21 
2015). Women workers’ workplaces are not free from sexual harassment which ranges from 
simple insults and demeaning remark to unwelcome touching and grabbing. 
 
The Gender Assessment mainly focused on the existing gender scenario of the RMG sector 
Bangladesh and tried to identify the problems, gaps and potential measures to overcome 
those. For the growth and sustainability of the sector, integrating women’s participation and 
contribution is a major concern. In this project, survey was conducted in 20 RMG units of 
different size, location & energy intensity to get an overview of the baseline scenario and 
identify the existing issues for ensuring gender equity. Along with this, KII, FGD and 
stakeholder consultations were conducted to get an overall picture of the current gender 
dynamics of the RMG sector in Bangladesh. 
 
5.2 Labor Force Participation 
Women in the labor force have been at the forefront of Bangladesh’s recent successful 
economic growth. They make up most of the workers in the ready-made garment (RMG) 
sector, the country’s flagship export-oriented industry. The creation of a large microfinance 
system focused on women—a subject of much study and emulation around the world—has 
also changed the economic position of women. Some of the major issues behind the 
participation of women in the RMG sector in Bangladesh are the following according to a 
study7: 
a) Women from extreme poor households have a significantly higher probability of 
participation compared to non-poor. The difference between moderate poor and non-
poor are insignificant. 
b) Poorer women are more likely to participate in low paid wage employment whereas 
the non- poor participate in self-employment. 
c) Presence of young children has a positive effect on self-employment and a negative 
effect on wage employment. 
d) Being married has a negative effect. This result may have been influenced by the fact 
that paid employment dominated by the RMG sector requires long hours of work. The 
study finds that there is a growing acceptance of outside employment among young 
unmarried workers.  
 
In socioeconomic context of Bangladesh, women participate less on formal livelihood activities 
in rural areas. Even if they do, there is discrimination in wages compared to men and socially 
it is not seen positively that a woman is working outside home. Also, there is limited livelihood 
opportunity due to lack of industries in rural areas. So, women from rural areas prefer RMG 
sector as there is good opportunity for women even with no education or skill, they get equal 
or almost equal pay as men, it is a formal employment and they get a regular salary each 
month. Moreover, they move to new places for work and there they can live independently 
and without judged by the society. On the other hand, as the women coming from the rural 
area are comparatively less educated or skilled than those of urban areas, the employers can 
hire them with minimum wages. Also, they are more vulnerable, docile and manageable than 
women in urban areas. They accept without protest the flexible terms of employment and 
hardly raise their voice against anything. This is why employers prefer to hire them.  
 
7 Bridges, S; Lawson, D; Begum, S. 2011. “Labour Market Outcomes in Bangladesh: The Role of Poverty and 
Gender Norms”, European Journal of Development Research, Vol. 23, www.palgrave-journals.com/ejdr. 
191

=== PAGE 195 ===
22 
 
Even though the RMG sector has played a tremendous role for women empowerment, there 
has been discrimination in labour force participation in this sector. From data collected within 
factories, 4 out of every 5 production line workers are female, whilst just over 1 in 20 
supervisors is a woman.8 This is mainly due to lack of education, trainings, technical 
knowledge and social responsibilities of women towards their families. Also, women are 
engaged more in sewing or as helpers compared to men. Less number of women are seen in 
RMG units working in cutting, quality control or supervising compared to men. A recent study9 
showed that Male workers are found to be less in helper and sewing department by about 
86% and 78% respectively.

According to the labor force survey (LFS), the female labor force participation rate increased 
from 2003 to 2006 and then increased dramatically from 2006 to 2010, reaching 36.1%. The 
LFS in 2013, found women’s participation declined to 33.5%, or a 2.6 percentage point fall 
between the two surveys.11 According to a recent survey of Center for Policy Dialogue (CPD), 
the ratio of female worker participation in the RMG sector, known as a women-driven sector, 
has seen a decline in recent years. According to the survey, there are 3,596 active RMG 
factories in Bangladesh with 3.5 million workers, of which 60.8% are female and 39.2% are 
male. In the previous survey in 2015, the workers were 36% male and 64% female. The survey 
was conducted on 2,270 workers in 193 factories in small, medium, and large categories in 
Dhaka, Gazipur, Narayanganj, and Chittagong.12  
 
During this study it was observed that the percentage of female workers is less than male 
workers. This scenario might have occurred because of the recent increase in wages which 
 
11 https://www.adb.org/sites/default/files/publication/203906/women-work.pdf 
12 https://cpd.org.bd/declining-female-participation-in-rmg-sector-cpd-study/ 
193

24 
attracted more male workers to join the labour force in RMG sector. Also, the employers are 
now becoming interested to hire male workers over females as they would not have to provide 
additional facilitates (i.e., maternity leave, special support for pregnant or lactating mother, 
workplace safety during night shifts) to men. Moreover, men are available anytime as they do 
not have family bindings or security related concerns in the perspective of Bangladesh.  
 
 
Figure 5-2 Gender distribution of workers in selected RMG units 
The RMG units were selected were based on their technical performance mainly and the 
survey was conducted with the Facility Managers to get an overall idea about the factory. 
However, in case of facility managers, all the facility managers in the sample are males. There 
was no representation of women in the higher management of the selected RMG units as well. 
This is a very common scenario in Bangladesh as the participation of women in managerial 
posts in garments sector is negligible. According to a study conducted on women workers in 
Bangladesh RMG sector by CPD in 2018, only about 0.5% of managers in RMG enterprises 
are female, and only about 9.3% of HR managers in the RMG sector are female. On the 
production floor, of the 60.8% females, more women are employed in the sewing section 
(73.9%) and less women work in the cutting section (22.7%).13

Lack of training facilities, skill and proper knowledge of technology are the main reasons for 
female workers to lag behind in the sector, the surveys reveal. Besides, male workers are 
taking more interests in the sector due to increasing wages. During FGD, female workers said

that there are trainings for workers but women do not participate because they feel most of

It was revealed from KII with experts that in terms of gender equality, the sector has very low 
wage discrimination at similar work-levels although some works are exclusive terrains of male 
workers. The sector does, however, has limited participation from women in decision-making 
positions - only 7% of management professionals are women. This leads to entry barriers in 
terms of market perceptions. Also, women themselves are also less inclined to get into such 
job roles. 
 
The tapering off the number of female workers can be stopped only by organizing training and 
skill development works, both by public and private organizations, the experts said. Over 3.31 
million people are, at present, working in this sector. Majority of them are laborer. Earlier in 
2013, the BBS survey found the percentage of male workers in the RMG sector 43.14 while 
the female workers 56.86%. The total workers in this sector were 2.99 million at that time. The 
reason of the decline is mainly due to changing technology, women have no opportunity to 
rule the readymade garments sector by utilizing sewing skill only. A comparison between the 
findings of the two BBS surveys also revealed that the average growth rate of employment of 
male workers in the RMG sector has increased 11.54% between 2013 and 2016-2017. On the 
other hand, the rate of employment for women has decreased by 6.56% during the same 
period. 
 
Specifically, on women workers in Bangladesh RMG sector, the CPD study reveals that only 
about 0.5% of managers in RMG enterprises are female, and only about 9.3% of HR managers 
in the RMG sector are female. On the production floor, of the 60.8% females, more women 
are employed in the sewing section (73.9%) and less women work in the cutting section 
(22.7%). The study points to the skill level comparison between males and females in terms 
of knowledge of using machines, and finds that compared to male workers, female workers 
are proportionately less knowledgeable about operating different machines. 
 
There are numerous estimates on the number of factories and number of labourers present. 
The list below gives the estimates from reputed establishments: 
a. Department of Inspection for Factories and Establishments (DIFE): DIFE has 
published a full database of 4841 RMG factories in 2017 (DIFE 2017). According to 
this data base, 2,155,453 workers are currently employed in the RMG industry of them 
43% are men and 57% are women.  
b. Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA): As 
per BGMEA documentation, there are 4,482 RMG factories employing about 4 million 
workforces of them 3.2 million are women (BGMEA, 2017).  
c. Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics (BBS): BBS conducts Labour Force Survey which 
is counted as having the biggest sample sizes in Bangladesh. According to this survey, 
there are 3.1 million workforces employed in the RMG factories of them 1.7 million are 
men and 1.4 million are women (QLFS, 2017) i.e. 54% and 46% men and women 
respectively.  
 
Government policies can affect female labor force participation rates, positively or otherwise. 
Some important policies are already in place and appear to have had good effect. The various 
195

=== PAGE 199 ===
26 
programs that have been implemented for increasing female enrollment in primary and 
secondary schools have been notably effective in eliminating the gender education gap. 
Bangladesh already has a relatively generous maternity leave policy. Employers are required 
to provide 16 weeks of paid maternity leave, as specified by the 2006 Labor Law. While in 
principle this policy is likely to increase female participation rates, it is unclear how much 
impact it has had in practice. It is unlikely to have any effect at all on anything but formal paid 
employment, which limits it to a small minority of working women. No systematic evaluations 
appear to have been made, but there are reports that paid maternity leave is generally not 
granted at most RMG factories and that most workers are not aware of their rights in this 
regard. 
 
5.3 Constrains for Female Employment 
The KIIs were conducted with policy makers, BGMEA, BKMEA, RMG factories, development 
partners, noted economists and asked mainly about their views on the present status of 
women in the RMG sector, the challenges of female employment and the strategies that can 
be adopted to raise female labour force participation in Bangladesh.14 
 
Figure 5-4 presents the seven major constraints to employing women as mentioned by the 
stakeholders. These constraints include lack of education, required skill and knowledge, social 
and work place safety, social norms, maternity and pregnancy issues, long working 
hours/overtime, lack of transport facilities and the care burden. 
 
Male-female equal contribution is very important for social coherence and balance. However, 
due to social constrains, female workers have face problems and sometimes have to sacrifice 
their career. After the updated increased PayScale, more men are showing their interest to 
join RMG sector. The owners also prefer to employ a male worker rather than a female worker. 
This is mainly because of – 
o Lack of skill in female workers. 
o Female workers need more breaks after marriage or after having children.  
o Safety issues need to be assured for female workers and mostly they do not want 
to stay for night shifts. 
 
Most of the female workers in the RMG sector are very young, 18-29 years and do not tend 
to work in the sector for a long period. This is mainly due to occupational hazard, health issues, 
damage to eyesight, having children etc. Also, employers do not prefer aged experienced 
workers in the factory to reduce cost and prefer to higher fresh workers at lower wages.  
 
However, social and gender issues are better than previous because maternity leave, and 
other facilities are given to the workers in most of the RMS units. 
 
14 Annex 1 presents a list of the KII participants 
196

=== PAGE 200 ===
27 
Figure 5-4 Major constraints to female employment perceived by different stakeholders 
From social perspective, male ratio is higher in management sector. It will be solved by 
creating awareness and providing training programs. There are some barriers like mind set 
up, lack of equity, lack of awareness, etc. It will be solved in future. BKMEA also provide 
lactating mother training (who have two children under 2 years age) and get 900 take-ups per 
month (total 36000 take-ups in two years) for extra care of mother (Government is paying the 
amount) and also female pregnant workers get maternity leave.  
Currently, rate of educated women has increased in the RMG sector with the increased 
education rate in the country overall. Young women joining the sector have mostly completed 
at least primary education with a good percentage of completion of eighth grade and above 
as well. However, women usually get less opportunity for technical skill development trainings 
due to the common perspective of the management that men are technically sounder than 
women and can learn new skills easily. This situation makes women lag behind from their 
male competitors and they cannot get promotions. In many cases, women have to be stagnant 
in a position for years or lose their job. To ensure increased women’s engagement, mandatory 
gender-sensitive trainings for all workers is needed. In this way female workers will not be 
dropped off for automation only due to lack of trainings.  
Jobs in the garments sector are divided among several sections and there are different 
workers for each section. At initial level the workers do not need much technical skills. But in 
later stages higher technical knowledge is required for operating modern machineries. No 
worker, both male and female enter into the sector knowing the technical skills; they gradually 
learn those after several trainings. The problem lies in gender inequality because male 
workers are given more opportunities and involved in decision making than female workers.

So, it is essential to ensure that both women and men get access to skill development

mechanism will be at place so that it can be assessed whether the management is addressing 
issues to ensure women empowerment and gender equality. 
5.4 Educational Status 
In RMG sector, at the worker level recruitments are done through interview and by skill test of 
the worker. No standard certification system exists to assess the skill level of the worker. RMG 
employers prefer at least class 5 level education while recruit workers and at least class 8 for 
the technical occupations and supervisors. According to a survey of Center for Policy Dialogue 
(CPD), released on Mar’18, among garment worker 60.8% are female & 39.20% are male. 
The survey was conducted on  2270 workers in 193 factories in Dhaka, Gazipur, Narayanganj 
& Chottogram. These large portions of women are mostly less or uneducated. 
Among the female workers surveyed, most have attained some form of education (SSC or 
HSC level, primary level or education up to class ten) and 4% have no formal education at all. 
Figure 5-5 Education Attainment of the Female Workers 
Among the male workers surveyed, there is a greater incidence of individuals with higher 
educational qualifications (34% have SSC qualification, 14% have HSC qualification, 9% have 
primary education attainment, 29% have education attainment up to class ten and 14% have 
a diploma or a bachelor’s degree) and there are no individuals without any formal education.

In RMG sector the practice of formal training is still not so popular. Only technicians and 
managerial staff undergo some formal training system but for the workers and supervisors the 
skills are achieved mostly through informal training which is well known as on-the-job training. 
Only a small number of entrepreneurs have been investing for developing the skills of their 
workforce. 
The apparel industry in Bangladesh needs to draw the attention of the youth of the country, to 
show them the huge career opportunities that are to be found in the RMG sector. This could 
include a stronger emphasis on providing young people with a basic understanding of the 
sector, including the various levels of employment opportunities at the floor level, to 
merchandisers, designers, product developers and beyond. 
In education, there is a strong need for greater levels of knowledge transfer between education 
and business. Many western countries have clear and well-developed knowledge transfer 
partnerships in place which sees ideas developed in colleges and universities being shared

with the industry. Bangladesh also needs to think carefully about how it can develop an 
education system to provide the RMG sector with the industry leaders of the future who will 
allow the sector to compete at the higher end of the value chain and address the need for 
competent middle management. It is unfortunate that the RMG industry in Bangladesh often 
hires mangers from neighboring countries such as Sri Lanka and India when there is an 
abundance of potential talent domestically available—if only they were nurtured the right way. 
Around 13 percent of the country’s garments factories have employed foreign experts in the 
top posts who remit over USD 5 billion from Bangladesh every year (CPD 2018). In the 
absence of a skilled workforce, particularly in merchandising, design and marketing, as 
well as in operation of sophisticated machines, the factory owners have hired experts 
from  China, Japan, India, and Pakistan to fill the gap. Out of 126 universities (46

universities should also include courses relevant to textile/RMG sector as it is a potential 
industry in the country.  
Moreover, current industry leaders have a vital role to play here, visiting schools and 
colleges and providing input to government agencies about the long-term needs of the RMG 
industry. There is an urgent need to develop a cadre of upper level managers. The country's 
business schools, in cooperation with BGMEA and BKMEA can develop training 
programmes for lower level managers to become upper level managers. Bangladesh 
should stop dependence on expatriate managers. Producing high skilled workers for the 
RMG industry will contribute to the economy by reducing the amount of foreign remittance 
that leaves the country.13 
At present the demand for skilled workers is increasing. Besides, it is becoming difficult 
for new women to get involved in this sector due to its labor-intensive nature of work. Women 
are quitting their jobs to take care of their children. Without initiatives taken at the public-
private level to make women skilled, it will be difficult for them to sustain in this profession. 
Moreover, Woman worker cannot work more than seven years on average. A number of 
experts and people involved with the sector said a large section of women workers quit 
jobs after having a child. 
5.5 Gender Perspectives of Energy Efficiency 
Different opinions were gathered on gender perspectives of energy efficiency from 
the stakeholder engagement workshop where a wide range of energy efficiency 
practitioners including regulators, policy makers, experts, academicians, researchers and 
garment sector entrepreneurs, as well as environment, social and gender experts 
participated. During the discussion it was identified that the women who are joining 
mainly are either unskilled, uneducated, or has joined without any training. As a result, no 
expertise is being created. With increased automation and use of advanced technology in 
machineries, already a decreasing trend of female workers is seen in the past 4 years. 
Experts are concerned that with the introduction of energy efficient machineries and 
equipment the percentage of female workers 
15 Rahman, S. 2019. Are educational institutions failing the RMG industry? The Daily Star. November 20, 2019. 
https://www.thedailystar.net/opinion/news/are-educational-institutions-failing-the-rmg-industry-1829314  
200

=== PAGE 204 ===
31 
might reduce even more if they cannot compete with the male workers in terms of technical 
knowledge and skills. Some recent studies also showed similar types of findings.  
 
According to a study, it was underlined that women workers are more vulnerable than their 
male counterparts to losing their jobs due to automation in 5 key sectors in Bangladesh 
including the readymade garment industry. It said women in low-wage occupations with less 
education are likely to be impacted more due to high risk of automation in readymade garment 
and textile, furniture, agro-processing, leather and footwear and tourism and hospitality 
sectors at the beginning of Fourth Industrial Revolution (4IR).16 The research also mentioned 
that the workers with primary education are going to be more vulnerable than those having 
secondary level of education. To overcome this situation, more trainings and knowledge 
sharing activities need to be promoted especially for the female workers as they are generally 
the ones with lower level of education and technical skills compared to the male workers in 
the context of Bangladesh.  
 
Another issue identified during stakeholder consultation was considering special needs of men 
and women while installing any energy efficient equipment. For instance, replacing regular 
lights with energy efficient lights is a very common measure towards attaining energy 
efficiency. However, some studies by BKMEA showed that workers face difficulties with color 
shading when LED lights are used and it ultimately caused eyesight damage especially to 
women as more women are engaged in sophisticated tasks like sewing in RMG sector. So, 
there should be some standard for choosing energy efficient machinery and equipment has 
no negative impact on health. 
 
5.6 Decision Making 
Bangladesh RMG sector plays a significant role to reduce poverty and to increase women 
empowerment. RMG industry has been swaggering in the country since long time as a catalyst 
for sustainable development and growth. Apart from job opportunities, the RMG sector gave 
underprivileged women to step out of their homes and live independently. Through working in 
the sector, women became more aware of their rights and started to raise voice for equality. 
Also, higher education can give increased wage which encouraged a lot of poor and 
underprivileged women to complete secondary (SSC) and higher secondary (HSC) level of 
education. With the job women can support their families financially and have secured a place 
in decision making at family level. Moreover, this sector plays a significant role in changing 
mindset of women as these working women realize the importance of education and tend to 
spend more on their children’s better education. 
 
However, there is still a gap in terms of decision making at the workplace. Workers’ 
organization/committees of different forms are available at workplaces but most of the women 
workers generally are not members of the organization/committees available at workplaces. 
Moreover, majority are not members of any organization/association at workplace. 
 
 
16 https://bd.apparelresources.com/business-news/sustainability/automation-weighs-heavy-women-garment-
workers/  
201

=== PAGE 205 ===
32 
While women workers are increasingly becoming representatives at the factory level, they are 
still not being equally represented. Also, leadership positions in union confederations and 
federations are still largely dominated by men. 
 
This is partly due to widely-recognized gender ideologies, as well as to the reality that women 
are still expected to assume the majority of childcare responsibilities after they return home, 
discouraging them from serving as trade union representatives. 
 
5.7 Wage and Benefits 
In every five year, the government forms a new Minimum Wage Board for the RMG sector to 
formulate a new wage structure for apparel workers. The board is usually led by a senior 
district judge as chairman. In addition to representative from the owners and workers in the 
board, it also consists of three independent members. The representatives of owners and 
workers separately propose minimum wage for the workers respectively and place it to the 
board in black and white. The government of Bangladesh gazette the new wage structure for 
RMG workers setting Tk 8,000 or USD 97.7117  as the minimum monthly pay in 2019.

The survey results showed that in RMG sector there is no gender-based discrimination in 
terms of wages and other benefits. In terms of gender equality, the sector has very low wage 
discrimination at similar work-levels although some works are exclusive terrains of male 
workers. Also, there is fair compensation provided to workers as per their pay grades. Most of 
the workers generally get festival bonus. Women workers get lunch break. However, except 
lunch break workers are not provided with opportunity to take rest. 
  
However, knowledge among the women workers about calculation of overtime compensation 
is poor. Three in every four women do not know the calculation method. So, the authority can 
manipulate them easily. Also, there is a discrimination in disbursement of salary on time. 
According to government policy, salary of the workers should be cleared within seven days 
after the due date. But in case of female workers there is often a delay in salary disbursement. 
Moreover, on terms of trainings and other opportunities women are les promoted. As a result, 
they get less promotions compared to their male colleague due lack of skill and technical 
knowledge. This is one of the reasons why there are few women in the supervisor and 
managerial positions.  
 
5.8 Workplace Environment 
The study also revealed that, overall working conditions for women is favorable - wage 
discrimination on the basis of gender in the sector is largely absent, special benefits are 
provided for women with special needs (like infrastructure for lactating mothers, maternity 
leaves etc.). The incidence of workplace harassment does exist and in case any such cases 
are reported, there is a robust redress mechanism in place. Also, examples of child labor are 
also limited.  
 
However, the scenario is only present in large scale factories and mostly absent in medium 
and small-scale factories. The workers from medium and small factories still suffer from 
unhealthy working condition, not having standard working hours, inadequate breaks during 
working hours and inadequate worker safety. Workplaces of the women workers are not free 
203

=== PAGE 207 ===
34 
from harassment. In other words, women workers experience different forms of harassment 
at workplace. Of the different forms, verbal abuse/harassment is the most common one. The 
reason behind this contrasting level of compliance between large scale and medium/small 
scale units is the growing concern of the buyers in social, health, safety and gender 
considerations after the Rana Plaza incident.  
 
Sexual harassment has decreased in the garments sectors in recent years due to strict rules 
and monitoring at the factories. Most of the large enterprises are highly compliant ensuring 
safety and security of their workers and there is functional sexual harassment complaint 
committee. In case of small and medium enterprises, there are sexual harassment complaint 
committee on paper, but their functionality and transparency are questionable. However, in all 
cases the workers have less trust on the committees and most of the time do not file any 
complaint.  So, this issue can be considered as a moderate risk factor as there is already 
existing committees for sexual harassment and the rate of sexual harassment is decreasing.  
 
Post Rana Plaza, there was a severe market and media pressure on the buyers i.e. large 
apparel brands to fulfil their social responsibility and ensure better working conditions for the 
workers employed in these manufacturing units. Accordingly, the buyers demanded stringent 
compliance with safety and health norms from the manufacturing units and would withdraw 
business in case these norms are not met. The Accord18 and Alliance19 was developed and 
strictly implemented for this purpose – audits were conducted, and the unit were notified on 
the areas of improvement. This prompted the large industries, who are in direct business with 
the buyers, to comply with these norms. However, the trickledown effect on small and medium 
scale units was limited since they rarely have forward linkages with the final buyers and mostly 
supply inputs for these large-scale units. Hence, there is a large scope of improving the social, 
health and safety conditions for the medium and small-scale units.  
 
Women workers are mostly deprived of social protection related facilities at workplace. High 
prevalence of ignorance/unawareness among workers concerning the social protection 
facilities are a striking feature in this regard for a long time. However, the situation is changing 
now. At present the factories try to comply with existing regulations and provide facilities to 
female workers.  
 
 
18 The Accord is an independent, legally binding agreement between brands and trade unions to work towards 
a safe and healthy garment and textile industry in Bangladesh. 
19 The Alliance for Bangladesh Worker Safety (Alliance) is a legally binding, five-year commitment to improve 
safety in Bangladeshi ready-made garment (RMG) factories. 
204

=== PAGE 208 ===
35 
From this study it was also observed that most factories try to provide maternity leave and 
special provisions for pregnant and lactating mothers as shown in the graph below.  
Figure 5-9: Workers’ Responses on Maternity Care 
 
In terms of safety issues, there is a risk of fire hazard as most of the materials and chemicals 
used in RMG are flammable. So proper fire safety needs to be ensured. In terms of health and 
safety issues this sector has improved a lot, especially after the Rana Plaza incident. However, 
they are still not up to the standard. The study found that among the total respondents, 48% 
said that they received trainings on chemical handling and storage of which 15% were female 
and 33% male workers. On the other hand, 52% said that they did not receive any training of 
which 21% were female and 31% were male (Figure 6-11).

The study revealed that among the respondents 66% received training on environment, heal 
and safety issues of which 21% were female and 45% were male. Rest of the 44% 
respondents said that they did not receive any sorts of training related to this issue from their 
facility. 
 
The effectiveness of initiatives undertaken by Government to address environment, social and 
gender related issues in RMG sector is high. But the owners are not always interested to follow 
those unless the initiatives are imposed on them. However, the RMG sector is doing 
comparatively better than other industries. Monitoring bodies like Accord, Alliance, ILO push 
the owners to follow the standards for which the current situation in terms of compliance have 
206

=== PAGE 210 ===
37 
improved. But there is still a lack of awareness among the workers about their rights in terms 
of wages and other facilities. So, they need to sensitize more so that they can be aware of 
their rights.  
 
5.9 Social beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes 
Participation of women is increasing in the Bangladeshi labor market, but socio-culturally 
constructed norms envisage that women and men’s position is not equal. Women tend to be 
concentrated in certain low-paying, low-skilled sectors such as the garment industry, and 
within it, are often stuck in lower paying occupations such as sewing machine operators and 
helpers. Due to stereotypes regarding women’s and men’s aspirations, preferences and 
capabilities, employers’ perceptions of women’s and men’s skills and attitudes are affected. 
This trend has been strengthened over the last two decades: as garment production becomes 
more technology-intensive, occupations become increasingly skilled and better paid, 
employing a higher proportion of men. Similar scenario was observed from the KII that female 
workers are decreased in Bangladesh in the RMG sector mainly because of – 
o Increased wage structure attracts male workers to this job and owners prefer male 
over female. 
o Due to automation and lack of skill in female workers. 
o Female workers need more breaks after marriage or after having children. This is 
also a reason why owner prefer male workers more. 
o Safety issues need to be assured for female workers and mostly they do not want 
to stay for night shifts. 
 
Moreover, most of the female labors in the garment industries are mainly involved in cutting, 
sewing, hemming, stitching, ironing, buttoning, and folding20. This is obviously seen as a 
gender-based skills compared to men whereas man mainly occupy supervisor or managerial 
positions. At the same time, men are firstly introduced to formal training, new technologies, 
and fashions. Women workers whom called “unskilled” are identified as marginal and low-paid 
labors and excluded from organizational and managerial training. Thus, women are 
marginalized in managerial roles because gender stereotypical beliefs and practices. 
 
Like many other countries, the participation of women in market economy especially in the 
managerial position of any kind of business firms in Bangladesh is seriously affected on limits 
by gender nature or stereotypes. Nevertheless, when men and women are in similar 
professions, clearly men’s earning, wages and profit is higher than women. In Bangladesh 
although government job’s payment system is equal for men and women, but non-government 
jobs salary is varied for men and women, especially in managerial position. Male than female 
mainly occupies educational institutions, bank, and insurance companies’ senior positions, 
which represent that strong gender stereotypes exist in the organizations. Besides these, 
women’s pregnancy and child rearing are further barrier to achieving stable or top most 
 
20 Habiba Z (2001) Paid Work and Socio-Political Consciousness of Garment Workers in Bangladesh. Journal of 
Contemporary Asia 31: 145-160. 
207

=== PAGE 211 ===
38 
position of an organization21. Trade Union is still dominated by male, whereas women’s 
position is as a recipient22,23. 
 
It was observed from the KII and field survey that there is less participation of women in 
management and technical positions. From social perspective, male ratio is higher in 
management positions and roles. It will be solved by creating awareness and providing 
training programs. There are some barriers like mind-set or cognitive biases against women, 
lack of equity, lack of awareness, etc. These are expected to be reduced over a period of time.   
 
5.10 Access to Information  
There is wide variation of in practice of leave provisions. Among several types of leave weekly 
holiday is the most enjoyed. Many workers are even unaware about the practice of leave at 
their workplaces. Night duty is mandatory for many women workers. In other words, when 
night duty is scheduled these workers are left with not options but to do night duty. There are 
workers who lack awareness in this regard. Lack of awareness has also been among many 
workers regarding practice of taking written consent from workers for night duty. However, 
largest segment of the workers claim that employers never take written consent from them.  
 
Not all employers generally do provide workers with information regarding risks at workplace. 
One in every four workers claims that their employers do not inform workers in this regard. 
RMG workers who have faced/observed accidents (pricking of needle during work, cutting 
finger or hand, fracturing leg and fracturing hand, fire) at workplaces are quite high. 40.4 
percent workers have this experience. There are also workers, though few, who don’t know 
whether any accident took place at their workplaces. Most workplaces maintain/have made 
available different facilities/arrangement for ensuring workplace safety. However, it also true 
that still some factories have not made enough arrangement. Many workers do not know 
whether organizations/committees exist at workplace. 
 
Presently, most of the garments have their own sexual harassment policies and any issues 
regarding sexual harassment is dealt with strictness. So, number of cases of sexual 
harassment has decreased in recent years. However, in many cases the victims do not file 
complaint due to lack of trust for getting justice or they feel embarrassed. Also, they do not 
know whether there is a system to file compliant or whom to approach first and. What are the 
procedures. To address these issues, sexual harassment should be added during the 
grievance redressal. Also, awareness of the workers should be increased about the 
committees and the complaint filing process.

5.11 Women and Health 
According to BLA, employers must ensure basic standards for health and safety in the 
workplace. In the Bangladesh labour Law-2006, specified the rights of health and hygiene in 
the section (51, 52, 53, 54, 56, 57, 59, and 60). Despite the specific code in the sections, 
health and safety violations are common in garment factories. Women in Bangladesh are more 
disadvantaged group than men in terms of health and nutrition. In most cases, due to closed 
windows, light and air is not sufficient in RMG factories. Toilets are not sufficient for the crowd. 
So, toilet remain dirty all the time proper water supply can’t properly supplied harmful gas 
emission from the toilet for the huge crowd and the environment of factory remains stinky most 
of the time.24 
 
Most of the female RMG workers live in urban slums or semi slums where clean drinking 
water, hygiene and sanitation health care are inaccessible due to scarcity and their low wages. 
Also, study identified that, community diseases are common in the workers live in slums. The 
female workers working in fabrics section (stitching, coloring, dye etc) are exposed to toxic 
elements and inhale dust of fabric which cause different breathing and respiratory diseases 
and those who are working. The female RMG workers mostly suffer from malnutrition, anemia, 
gastric, dysentery, hepatitis, diarrhea, respiratory problem, gynecological problems, 
tuberculosis and urine infection etc. Other than these diseases as they are sitting for a long 
working hour lead them to suffer from bones such as neck pain, joint pain, musculoskeletal 
pain, neural problem and problem in body muscles, joint, tendons, ligaments and bones. Also, 
the noisy environment from sewing machines cause hearing disability.25  The female RMG 
workers receive only BDT 200 as medical allowance as per wages boards, 2010 but their 
average monthly medicine expense is BDT 497.5, which is more than double what they are 
provided.26 
 
Few RMG workers get the day care and in many garment factories, mothers do not have 
space or time to breastfeed. Breastfeeding rates by working women in garment factories can 
be as low as 10 percent (UNICEF 2015). Workplaces of these workers are not gender 
sensitive— there are insufficient numbers of toilets for women; no arrangement of sanitary 
napkins at workplace; and most of the factories also do not arrange women doctors.27 
Unwanted physical contact and sexual harassment by lineman, supervisor and other male 
workers lead to sexual diseases as well as hamper mental wellbeing. Moreover, female 
workers abort babies to retain their job due to their poor economic condition. 
 
5.12 Access to Financial Services by RMG Workers 
Even though the majority of the RMG workers are female, their wages are not up to mark to 
satisfy them even after working for a long time (including overtime). Consequently, they do not

have access to basic needs such as nutritious food or better clothing, medications etc. A study 
conducted on female Garment workers in Chittagong Metropolitan area identified the insecure 
condition of the female workers who are still trapped in the vicious cycle of poverty.28  
 
A recent study conducted by Care Bangladesh indicates that female RMG workers want to 
use financial services, for benefits that include loans, savings, their own bank/Bkash29 
accounts, etc.30 According to the IFC data, there is a stark gender gap in access to financial 
services in Bangladesh, where only 36% of women have a formal bank account, compared to 
65 percent of men.31 The study also indicated that many female RMG workers may not have 
full control over their earnings. Thus, financial services that enables a female RMG worker to 
exercise greater management of her finances may empower her and facilitate greater savings 
and more financial control. Furthermore, due to the limited amount of wages, they cannot save 
much for the future. 
 
Given the economic significance of the readymade garment sector, which is dominated by 
female workers, digitizing wage payments through MFS can have a significant impact on 
women’s financial inclusion. Almost about 90% of factories pay their workers in cash, which 
is a missed opportunity for both the financial and RMG sectors. When garments workers open 
MFS accounts to receive their salaries, they can avail a broad range of financial services. 
Through IFC’s initiative, over 70,000 female workers benefitted from digital wage payments to 
their mobile financial services accounts between 2016 and 2018, and they can now use their 
accounts to send money, build savings and make payments securely. However, non-
availability of national ID of workers, lack of financial literacy and awareness about accounts, 
and difficulty in covering fixed costs incurred by MFS providers are among major constraints 
in wage digitization in readymade garment industry in Bangladesh.32  
 
5.13 Leadership Opportunities for Women in RMG Sector  
In Bangladesh, textile and garments sector is entirely male dominated. Even though the 
present elected president of BGMEA is a woman, there is no existing database on women 
enterprises or women involved in the higher management positions in the textile and garments 
industry.  
 
According to the Global Gender Gap Report 2020, companies with female-majority ownership 
in Bangladesh are only 1.70%, whereas firms with female top managers are 4.80%. During 
the gender assessment, no women led enterprise could be reached. According to a recent 
report, women in leadership and in organizational decision-making in businesses as well as 
in the company’s board are very few, because of social stigma towards women. Male

6 Gender Inclusion Practices in Bangladesh 
Equal rights for workers can be best assured by a combination of legislative measures as well 
as awareness of the business owners and their management about the advantages to be 
gained through employment of workers based on their capabilities rather than gender or other 
personal attributes. Recent government initiatives including amendments to the Labour Law 
in 2013 and Labour Rules in 2015 have made inroads into ensuring equality in the workplace, 
and the introduction of training initiatives including those related to effective workplace 
cooperation and Essentials of Occupational Safety and Health (EOSH) conducted by the 
BGMEA, BKMEA and BEF, with support from ILO-ITC since 2014, to establish a culture of 
occupational safety and health and respect at the factory level, go some way to improving the 
situation.33 
 
Equal responsibility within the RMG industry has been greatly helped by initiatives launched 
by the Bangladesh government and other bodies, which include the recently formed Tripartite 
Consultative Committee (TCC) that addresses labour and workplace related issues in the 
RMG sector and is a substantial step towards forming a collaborative and all-inclusive entity.34 
It was established specifically to deal with all unresolved or disputed issues within the 
workplace. Initiatives such as these, together with the work undertaken with the guidance of 
Accord and Alliance and other agencies, have created an environment in which ethical, 
environmental and sustainable standards are not only considered the norm, but also the 
minimum level that any RMG entity today has to abide by in order to gain necessary 
compliance certification from the customers. 
 
Promoting equal participation of men and women in trade unions can play a crucial role in 
ensuring rights and interests of workers especially raising women workers’ issues such as 
sexual reproductive health rights, sexual harassment, adopting a gender approach to OSH, 
leadership and communication skills.35 In addition, it also helps to mainstream gender issues 
in all policies and programmes, including collective bargaining to promote women workers’ 
rights. However, Trade unions’ activities must be focused, rights based, efficient and aligned 
with regulatory measures provided in the Bangladesh provisions provided in the Bangladesh 
Labour Act (2006) and the Labour Rules (2016). 
 
Bangladesh RMG industry is subject to the harshest scrutiny regarding all aspects of health 
and safety at work, compliance and structural standards following the tragic events of April 23, 
2013 at the Rana Plaza. The Accord, Alliance, and National Action Plan (NAP) initiatives, 
implemented since that fateful day, have ensured that the RMG sector has greatly improved 
and now has 7 of the 10 highest rated LEED factories in the world, complying with the highest 
ethical and environmental standards throughout the industry. Level of compliance is high for 
those industries which have realized the business need of complying with safety regulations. 
In this respect, Rana Plaza incident has completely transformed the market by highlighting the 
disastrous effect the lack of safety can have on not only the workers but also on overall 
business prospects of the RMG units. The key reasons for non-compliance with applicable 
 
33 https://www.thedailystar.net/opinion/perspective/enhancing-equality-within-rmg-industry-1573354  
34 https://www.dhakatribune.com/business/2017/03/12/govt-forms-tripartite-council-rmg-sector  
35https://www.ilo.org/wcmsp5/groups/public/---asia/---ro-bangkok/---ilo-

regulations would extra cost of compliance which in some cases are an added burden to the 
limited financial capacity. In current market scenario, with business margins getting 
constrained, this added cost is leading to disparity in competitive advantage – with non-
compliant companies having spare resources for creating market outreach. However, 
awareness about the benefits of compliance is increasing and it is expected levels of 
compliance will further increase in the days ahead, with market pressure increasing as well.  
 
However, the investment to achieve such improvements is not always recognized by the 
immediate customer, and there is an unhealthy culture that regards Bangladesh as a low-cost 
source, no matter what the price. Equality at this level surely demands that the buyers respect 
the investments and the advances made by the RMG industry in Bangladesh and also realize 
that these changes have come about through significant investment in both plant and 
machinery and that, in a world of escalating raw material costs, some price levels are artificially 
suppressed.

7 Legal and Administrative Framework Protecting and 
Promoting Gender Equality in Bangladesh 
 
Bangladeshi women have been struggling to establish their rights in family, society and in the 
state. In reality, women are still facing discrimination, exclusion and injustice and have a 
negligible influence in decision-making processes in a male-dominated society of Bangladesh. 
Discriminatory laws and policies hinder formal equality and socio-political conditions prevent 
women from exercising their rights.36 
 
The implementation of women development policy and work plan is the basic responsibility of 
the Government. This responsibility can be discharged on a sound basis through building up 
a well-organized and broad-based institutional system. Despite having under-representation 
of women at different levels, the government of Bangladesh has been implementing different 
steps to ensure gender equality and women’s empowerment in the country. Through the vision 
2021 and 2041, a momentum has been created for taking forward Bangladesh to a middle 
and high-income level, respectively. The GoB has undertaken various programs to materialize 
Vision 2021 for establishing rights of women, their empowerment and mainstreaming them in 
the overall development. The 7th Five-Year Plan (2016-2020) of the Bangladesh government 
considers women’s engagement in political and economic activities as a crosscutting issue 
and one of the main drivers of transformation. Realizing the essence of ensuring gender 
equality, the government has shown its commitment to attaining the Sustainable Development 
Goals (SDG) of gender equality and empowering women and implementing the Convention 
on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing 
Platform for Action.  
 
There is a dedicated ministry, the Ministry of women and Children Affairs (MoWCA) for the 
development and welfare of women in the country. Also, the Ministry of Labour and 
Employment (MoLE) that deals with labor related issues have particular focus on ensuring 
gender equality in the employment sector and to achieve that they promote different types of 
capacity building activities for different sectors. The MoWCA has been implementing different 
programs to alleviate poverty of women, stop abuse of women, stop trafficking of women, and 
ensure safety in all areas including their workplace with a view to ensuring full and equal 
participation of women in socio-economic activities. ILO and Ministry of Labour and 
Employment have taken initiatives to provide capacity building trainings and leadership 
programs to women working in RMG sector.37 BGMEA and BKMEA also have training and 
capacity development programs for both male and female workers. BGMEA also promotes 
potential female workers for obtaining higher education by providing them scholarships.  
 
Bangladesh has already substantially achieved the MDGs as it has achieved gender parity in 
primary and secondary education at the national level, among other successes of the MDGs. 
Bangladesh has been working relentlessly to ensure women’s overall development by 
ensuring their equal and active participation in the mainstream socio-economic activities and 
removing the various impediments to their empowerment. In order to attain the SDG goal of 
gender equality; the government of Bangladesh has been implementing different initiatives so

8 Alignment of the Project with GCF Gender Assessment 
Guideline 
 
According to GCF guideline, gender analysis needs to take place early in the planning process 
so that an understanding of gender roles and power relations is built into the project. All 
projects/programs should include an analysis of gender and include data on gender issues 
within the overall situation analysis. In this project survey was conducted in selected 20 RMG 
factories among 70 workers and 20 facility managers. The survey questions were based on 
educational qualification, labor force participation, participation in decision making process, 
existing facilities on the factory, gender considerations etc. 
 
The project reviewed the existing policies, strategies and frameworks related to labor 
participation especially considered gender aspects both at national and international level. 
The projects interventions will be designed considering the legal status of women as well. In 
addition, the project considered commonly held beliefs, perceptions, and stereotypes related 
to gender, the division of labor among women and men and the participation between women 
and men in the formal/informal economy in the project/program footprint area. Also, during the 
stakeholder engagement workshops, KII and factory surveys it was ensured that both male 
and female participants take part in the discussions and give their valuable opinion. However, 
RMG sector and associated sectors related to this has very little involvement of women in 
higher management, research or academic line. Still the study tried to ensure participation of 
female respondents as much as possible.  
 
During the first and second workshop 15% and 11% female participants were present 
respectively. The workshops were designed to share ideas around a wide range of energy 
efficiency practitioners including regulators, policy makers, experts, academicians, 
researchers and garment sector entrepreneurs, as well as environment, social and gender 
experts. There were participants from different ministries and government agencies, BGMEA, 
BKMEA, garments units along with development partners, NGOs, academic and research 
organizations who have been working with different issues related to RMG sector in 
Bangladesh, especially environmental, social and gender concerns.  
 
During KII, 25% respondents were women and 50% of the respondents had knowledge and/or 
experience on working with gender related issues in the RMG sector in Bangladesh. 
Especially, respondents from Ministry of Labour and employment (MoLE), Ministry of 
Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MoEFCC) and Economic Relations Division (ERD) 
under Ministry of Foreign Affairs shared their views and knowledge regarding gender 
consideration and its importance in the sustainability of RMG sector. Representatives from 
BGMEA and BKMEA had first-hand experience on gender issues and providing capacity and 
knowledge building training to both male and female workers, promote female workers with 
potential for higher education and training and provide trainings to the workers on health and 
safety related issues. The researchers and academicians were also directly involved in 
addressing gender issues in the RMG sector and working for gender equality within the sector. 
 
During factory survey more than 40% respondents were female workers. However, during the 
survey with facility managers there were no female respondents as the selected RMG units 
did not have any female facility managers till the survey was conducted. 
217

=== PAGE 221 ===
48 
 
Moreover, the project considered the existing gender inequalities and situation of women and 
men in the interventions related energy efficiency and emphasize more on capacity building 
on energy efficiency for both male and female workers. Also, access to financial resources 
and control over these resources is considered while designing the interventions. In addition, 
it will address differential needs/priorities of women and men in the context of the project. 
 
The project will ensure the following issues in the gender action plan: 
• 
Services and technologies provided by the project/program be available and accessible to 
both women and men 
• 
Equal access for the women and men from vulnerable communities to information and 
opportunities necessary to participate and benefit fully from the anticipated outcomes of 
the project  
• 
Ensure capacity development activities of disadvantaged groups 
• 
Encourage women entrepreneurship in RMG sector 
• 
Encourage more educated women to enter RMG sector in higher management 
• 
Promote leadership capacity of female workers 
 
The information gathered from the gender analysis/assessment should be considered in all 
stages of the project cycle: design, formulation, implementation, and monitoring and 
evaluation. In each of these stages, project managers should keep a ‘gender lens’ in mind, 
looking at ways the project/program can address gender inequalities that emerge from the 
project; ensure the differential needs of women and men are addressed; ensure women and 
men have equal access to resources, services, and capacity development; ensure equal 
participation of women and men in management arrangements and as beneficiaries, partners 
and key stakeholders; and ensure women’s equal participation in decision – making 
processes. 
 
Moreover, the Ministry of Women and Children’s Affair (MoWCA) and Ministry of Labour and 
Employment (MoLE) will be involved in the project activities. While developing the training 
modules for capacity building, consultations will be done with these respective ministries as 
well so that they can provide adequate guidelines. Also, experiences shared by the ministries 
regarding capacity building of industrial workers, especially underprivileged women will help 
to develop the proposed actions in a sustainable way.

9 Grievance Redressal System (GRS) 
 
Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) is recognized worldwide as one of the important 
indicators of an organization’s proficiency and efficiency. Grievance redressal is, in fact, a 
governance related management process. In this case, the GRM will help to identify whether 
the stakeholders as well as communities and households living near the factories are facing 
any negative impacts due the implementation and operation of the sub-projects.  
 
Management staff in the RMG sector come from varied backgrounds and do not necessarily 
have professional management trainings to understand and address issues related to well-
being of workers. So, the GRS need to easily manageable, easily accessible and user friendly.  
 
In this project the End Borrower would ensure that grievance redress procedures are in place 
and would monitor those procedures to ensure that grievance are handled properly. The End 
Borrower will establish a procedure to answer sub-project-related queries and address 
complaints, disputes, and grievances about any aspect of the sub-project, including 
disagreements regarding the assessment and mitigation of environmental and social impacts. 
Generally, the grievance redress committees (GRC) are of two types (i) formal courts of appeal 
and (ii) a locally constitutes GRC for dispute resolution. The second may not totally avoid but 
may reduce the problem significantly. Grievance Redress Committee (GRC) will be formed. 
The GRC will ensure proper presentation of complaints and grievances, as well as impartial 
hearings and transparent decisions. The GRCs will meet periodically to discuss the merit of 
each case and fix a date for hearing and notify the aggrieved persons to submit necessary 
documents in proof of her/his claim/case; resolve grievances within one month of receipt of 
complaint. Additional details regarding the functioning of GRC is presented in the SMF. 
 
A general guideline for the GRS will be developed by the LFIs in consultation with the MoWCA 
and BGMEA. It should be ensured that the GRM within the RMG units are aligned with the 
GCF Independent Redress Mechanism as well as the Accredited Entity’s Grievance Redress 
Mechanism (including contact details, accessibility and basic procedures of such 
mechanisms, etc.).  
 
Grievance Redress Committee (GRC) will be formed and established at each project site. 
Grievance response focal point will be available at each shelter site for instant response to an 
aggrieved person, receiving written complaints or suggestions, produce them to the GRC for 
hearing and resolution. The GRC will be comprise of the members from the PIU (Please refer 
Gender action Plan: Activity 3.1.1: Formulation of a project committee with women 
participating for monitoring of the activities under the program.)  and representatives from 
workers (one male and one female). This GRC will be responsible for reporting to the LFIs. 
Detailed structure and mechanism are described in the ESMF. The Grievance Redress 
Committee (GRC) will be same for ESMF & gender issue. 
 
The following issues should be considered during for a successful GRM: 
User manual: An easy to understand instruction document should be provided for the GRM 
for the general user wishing to avail the proposed central GRM. 
 
User friendliness: The system should not be too complicated either in technical terms or in 
language for the general public irrespective of education background. 
219

=== PAGE 223 ===
50 
 
End-to-end tracking: Tracking of grievance both by the complainant and the authority for 
grievance redressal is very important.  
 
Confidentiality, security and anonymity: Keeping complainant’s identity a secret is very 
important for security reasons. Ensuring that the system provides an option to complainants 
for lodging their grievances anonymously would also ensure confidentiality and security. 
 
Progress monitoring: Grievance should be addressed within 7 days of receiving. A 
grievance redress system is no good if the grievances are not addressed regularly. When the 
grievances are not addressed for a long time then the employees will lose faith from the 
system and stop sharing their issues. 
 
Feedback option: There should be a feedback option to measure client satisfaction and 
collect opinions on the effectiveness of the system.  
 
 
 
 
220

=== PAGE 224 ===
51 
10 Recommendations 
 
The issue of gender equality has immense significance as it is not only a fundamental human 
right but also a necessary foundation for a peaceful, prosperous and sustainable world.38 
Providing women and girls with equal access to education, health care, decent work, and 
representation in political and economic decision-making processes will fuel sustainable 
economies and benefit societies and humanity at large. Implementing new legal frameworks 
regarding female equality in the workplace and the eradication of harmful practices targeted 
at women is crucial to ending the gender-based discrimination prevalent in many countries 
around the world. Thus, several initiatives are required to be taken to augment the process of 
attainment gender equality in Bangladesh.  
 
In order to create a gender equitable environment at the workplace, the following initiatives 
can be implemented: 
• 
Create enabling environment for hiring workers considering their skills rather than gender. 
• 
Ensure job security by issuing job contracts and ensure effective implementation of 
different laws and regulations in terms of wages and other facilities. 
• 
Ensure trainings and capacity development workshops for both male and female workers. 
• 
Awareness raising activities among communities and families so that no woman has to 
leave their carrier due to social pressure or responsibilities. 
• 
Capacity development of female RMG workers to avail financial services and awareness 
on existing financial services. 
• 
Advocacy needs to be done with the family members to engage women in the family and 
community level economic activities so that women can be financially independent and 
take their own decisions. 
• 
Building a network among women, influential community and educational leaders is 
important so that the network would work as a catalyst by bringing changes in the society. 
• 
Provide safe transportation facilities especially for woman so that they can join work in 
different shifts as well as men without getting concerned about security issues. 
• 
Provide better medical and mental health facilities to workers for increasing their health 
which will eventually increase their performance. 
• 
Ensure special provisions for pregnant and lactating mothers and maternity leave should 
be as per government rules.  
• 
Provision of daycare facilities by the factory authority as well as community.  
• 
Facilitate gender responsive and socially inclusive training in the RMG units. 
• 
Initiatives to improve the women’s access to social services.  
• 
Take initiatives to foster equality in the decision-making process and encourage 
participation of both male and female workers in trade bodies or labour unions for 
developing better leadership skills. 
• 
Implement different programmes those would facilitate women’s access over resources.  
• 
Organize capacity-building events for industry personnel on gender sensitivity and equal 
opportunities. 
• 
Ensure proper occupational health and safety for all the workers irrespective of gender. 
• 
Ensure harassment free working environment for the workers regardless of gender. 
 
38 https://www.daily-sun.com/post/345296/SDGs-and-Gender-Equality-in-Bangladesh  
221

=== PAGE 225 ===
52 
11 Gender Action Plan 
11.1 Outline 
A project/program-specific Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a tool used to ensure gender 
mainstreaming is clearly visible in project/program design and implementation. The project 
GAP is not a separate component. It mirrors the logical framework of the project and is an 
integral part of project/program design. GAPs include clear targets, gender design features 
and measurable performance indicators to ensure women’s participation and benefits. Key 
aspects of the GAP are incorporated into project/program assurances to encourage buy-in 
from AEs and other partners.  
 
The GAP presents:  
• 
Work undertaken to address gender issues in the project/program;  
• 
Targets and design features, included in the project/program to address gender 
concerns and ensures tangible benefits to women and men, especially from vulnerable 
communities;  
• 
Mechanisms to ensure implementation of the gender design elements;  
• 
Gender - sensitive monitoring and evaluation indicators.  
 
Below is presented a GAP that contains impact, outcome and output statements, gender – 
responsive activities, gender – performance indicators and targets, timeline, responsibility 
lines, and approximate budgetary allocations against each activity.

11.2 Monitoring of Gender Action Plan 
Ensuring the sustainability of the gender action plan is a major concern and to achieve this a 
proper monitoring system should be in place. At the sub-project level, there will be Project 
Implementation Unit (PIU)39 who will be responsible for the proper implementation of the 
gender related interventions. The PIU will submit periodic monitoring reports (yearly) to the 
project committee in Activity 3.1.1: Formulation of a project committee with women 
participating for monitoring of the activities under the program. The LFIs will then monitor 
whether the end borrowers (i.e., RMG units) are following the policies and guidelines regarding 
gender issues and the action plan is implemented ensuring gender equality and women 
empowerment within the sector.  
 
The selected LFIs involved in the program have experience on monitoring gender related 
issues. Most of their RMG clients work with renowned international buyers who have certain 
conditions precedent on gender related matters. As a result, they have to maintain certain 
guidelines to ensure gender equality and women empowerment. Thus, the LFIs are well aware 
of the gender related concerns and are capable of monitoring the overall gender related 
activities during the 05 years of GCF loan availability period. 
 
For the Gender Action Plan of this programme, the LFIs have agreed to conduct onsite as well 
as offsite periodic inspection for ensuring proper practice of gender related issues in the 
projects. In the appraisal of loan proposal, they will assess the client’s willingness and ability 
to implement gender issues as required by GCF and other regulatory authorities. They are 
cooperative in terms of gender related concerns and they will establish a monitoring 
mechanism within their organization. The LFIs will conduct annual assessment after the 
project implementation throughout the 05 years of GCF loan availability period. During the 
assessment it will be assessed whether the end borrowers have implemented the gender 
issues mentioned in the gender action plan and created enabling environment of increased 
women empowerment. 
 
39 The structure of the PIU will be same as proposed in the ESMF.
