1. Introduction 
Government of Bangladesh committed to various energy consumption reduction targets in the past 
few years and initiated EE&C programmes like Energy Management Program, EE Labelling Program 
and EE Buildings Program, targets at large energy consuming entities and equipment in the industrial, 
residential and commercial sectors. Bangladesh being an international hub for clothing (textile and 
readymade garments production1), has one of the world’s fastest growing economy, largely driven by 
its exports in textile and readymade garments. The textile sector alone contributes to above 12% in 
GDP with more than 81% of the export earning coming from textiles & textile related products2. 
To meet the demands from world over, the sector has been growing and expanding rapidly over the 
last few years. With concentration of more production units, the electricity consumption in the sector 
is increasing and so is the employment of the workforce. The textile labour market has its own set of 
challenges like unorganised, uneducated-unskilled labour, urban-rural and wide male-female worker 
ratio disparities. Besides, the industrial challenges for the workforce are low wages, exhaustive long 
shifts, compromised health & safety, hazardous working conditions, sexual harassment/discrimination 
and lack of human resource policies.  
Evidently, deriving from the above-mentioned facts, gender inequalities affect more woman than men 
in this case, given the fact, women are more personally, socially, economically and politically 
challenged. The industrial units prefer women labourers, especially young women workers since they 
are in abundant supply at a very low cost. However, in textile units, women employment is less due 
to the manual, labour intensive processes3. Stereotypically it isn’t favoured by owners to employ 
women due to their low competence, productivity and capacity to perform certain tasks in textile 
units. Therefore, presence of women is very less and the rest few that are working in textile units as 
directors, managers or executives face unequal access to authority and decision-making, human and 
legal rights, house/asset ownership, bank credits, education and skill development, economic 
resources, and health services. 
Bangladesh’s Third National Communication (TNC)4 to UNFCCC clearly states that Bangladesh is one 
of the most climate vulnerable countries in the world and the challenges posed by climate change 
make the lives and livelihoods of millions of people increasingly complex and vulnerable. Bangladesh 
is prone to climate disasters like floods, droughts and cyclones. Consequently, climate change and its 
impacts are enhancing vulnerabilities for women due to the lack of climate adaptive capacities. 
Struggles in coping up with the impacts of climate disasters, living in extreme unhealthy working 
conditions, inability to earn a living due to health issues and lack of education and skills, fully covered 
drapes and clothes that reduce the possibility of escaping the place of climate event and man-made 
disasters like fire, are a few to name. 
To understand the delicate relation between energy efficiency and gender equality, there is a need to 
understand the capacity of energy efficient processes and equipment to influence the life of workers 
both socially and economically by deep diving into the socio-economic status of the textile workforce 
which determines the human and economic development in the past and the upon technology

1.1. 
Programme brief and relevance  
The programme ‘Promoting private sector investment through large scale adoption of energy saving 
technologies and equipment for textile sector of Bangladesh’ aims to reach out to the business 
establishments and entrepreneurs in textile sector to provide them with financial and market 
resources to avail investment opportunities for energy saving technology upgrades. The programme 
objectives have clearly been laid out to ensure direct and indirect benefits to the vulnerable and 
underprivileged sections of the society which are dependent on this sector. Project activities consider 
encouraging gender equality throughout the phases which particularly involves women in 
consultations, decision making and embedding gender specific benefits during the implementation of 
the programme. 
During the design and development of the programme, gender equality has been maintained at the 
stakeholder consultation and workshop, key informants at planning and management level (women 
in the ministries, relevant department and associations), executive and labour level. Women 
respondents have been included and preferred to answer the survey questions related to facilities and 
challenges at their workplaces (in different sections in textile units).

1.2. 
GCF’s guideline on gender equality 
The Green Climate Fund has a mandate to support a paradigm shift to low-emission and climate-
resilient development and gender mainstreaming is central to its objectives and guiding principles, 
through engaging women and men of all ages as stakeholders in the design, development and 
implementation of strategies and activities to be financed5. 
The Gender Policy and Action Plan was adopted in March 2015, by the GCF board. The policy states, 
that a gender-sensitive approach is therefore part of a paradigm shift; Impacts of climate change are 
borne differently by man and women and existing gender inequalities are likely to be exacerbated; 
greater vulnerability of women to climate change stems from gender norms and discrimination that 
result in the imbalanced division of labour, lower incomes, and lesser livelihood opportunities; less 
access and control over land and other productive assets; fewer legal rights; lesser mobility and lesser 
political and professional representation. 
1.2.1. Objective, Principles and Mandatory requirements 
The overall objective of the gender policy is to ensure that by adopting a gender-sensitive approach, 
the Fund will efficiently contribute to gender equality and achieve greater and more sustainable 
climate change results, outcomes and impacts6.  
It is a mandatory requirement of the gender initiatives under the GCF programme to satisfy the basic 
requirements i.e. gender equality and equity, inclusiveness, accountability, country ownership 
through alignment with national policies and priorities in stakeholder inclusion, enhancing 
competencies through fund’s institutional framework and equitable resource /allocation for equal 
benefits for men and women through fund’s climate action.

Figure 1: The Fundamental Principles of GCF's Gender Policy 
Mainstreaming gender in GCF programmes can be synthesized into an approach which mandates a 
gender analysis to assess social, economic and political factors underlying climate change-exacerbated 
gender inequality, action plans with methods and tools to promote equality, and gender sensitivity of 
the monitoring and evaluation of the programme to measure and quantify the outcomes and impacts 
of programme activities on women and men’s resilience to climate change.  
In line with GCF’s gender policy, IDCOL has conducted a gender assessment and has ensured gender 
balance during all activities conducted under the PPF for this programme. The gender specific needs 
and priorities of men and women have been assessed and taken up for an action plan as instructed by 
the GCF. 
2. Methodology 
Gender assessment is done to investigate gender roles and power relations in relation to the 
programme design and identify multiple causes of vulnerability, including gender inequality. It is a tool 
used to identify and build on the diverse knowledge and capacities within communities/households 
that can be used to make them more resilient to climate related shocks and risks.   
This document is based on the review of national data and statistics, progress reports and government 
published documents; websites (ministries, departments, associations, international agencies and 
other active organisations) and resources from authentic sources; Meetings and Key informant 
interviews with the stakeholders and gender specialists. The data and information obtained from the 
secondary sources is equated from the primary sources (site visit, field survey, worker and community 
interviews, etc). The industrial survey has been done with the a) Facility lead/CEO/MD; b) Facility 
engineer; c) Facility workers (male and female) d) Community. The textile facilities were picked 
through random sampling. Once selected, these factories were visited for the survey and the workers 
were picked through stratified random sampling. The workers were split through the Strata 1: Female 
and Strata 2: Male and then picked randomly. The sample was stratified in order to maintain a gender 
balance.

3. A contextual snapshot of Gender sensitivity and equality in textile sector in 
Bangladesh 
Textile sector being the major recruiter in Bangladesh has a men-women ratio of 70:307. Some of the 
textile mills don’t even have a single female worker.  The few employed women in the textile sector 
belong to the most vulnerable sections of the society. However, both women and men labour have 
increased vulnerability due to wage and income gaps, access to finance, lack of rights for land and 
property, access to information, health and safety issues8.  
International statistics show that Bangladesh has a Gender Inequality Index Rank of 1199  and a Global 
Gender Gap Index Rank 7210. These are the function of reproductive health (maternal mortality ratio 
and adolescent birth rate), empowerment (share of parliamentary seats held by women and share of 
population with at least some secondary education), and labour market participation (labour force 
participation rate).  
Hence, Bangladesh has a long way to go for which programmes that incorporate gender sensitive 
approach are vital. Development of this sector will have direct and indirect benefits for women 
currently employed and those that seek employment which will show up in a few years. 
3.1. 
Gender and energy efficiency in textile sector 
There exists a delicate relationship in gender and energy efficiency. It is essential to look at the trends 
of employment, which suggest, textile industry has more male workers than female. The employment 
of men is favoured in textile industry because of the common perceptions like processes in the textile 
units are labour intensive and manual, women can’t perform tasks which include heavy lifting, shifting 
and loading. Women experience occupational segregation, as most of which are employed are given 
lighter, sitting jobs. This leads to insufficient skill development and possibly lower wages for women 
employed in Bangladesh’s textile mills. 
The equipment and processes currently being used in textile sector in Bangladesh are labour and 
energy intensive. In most cases, smaller and medium textile units do not undertake energy audits and 
continue to use decade-old technologies. Quite evidently, lack of skills and use of age-old, labour-
intensive machinery are big constraints for job opportunities for women in this sector.  
In the past, buyers to bring down their costs, often overlooked the working conditions of their 
manufacturing labour class and exploited their skills for low wages. The government of Bangladesh 
has been making stringent rules and amendments 11in the existing laws to counter these challenges. 
Recently, it has been observed that the international buyers like M&S, H&M, Zara, Puma, etc., to meet 
 
7 Calculated from Primary sources of data: Field survey and KII with the facility managers 
8 International Labour Organisation (https://www.ilo.org/infostories/en-GB/Stories/Employment/barriers-
women#intro)

I 
their sustainability standards/commitments and CSR targets12, have started pushing the 
owner/suppliers to maintain certain standards in employee facilities and safety13. The textile mills 
have been pushed further to reduce the gender gaps in employment. To facilitate the mandate of 
their buyers, mills are undertaking initiatives like equal wages, child care centres, maternity and 
paternity leaves, medical facilities, safety precautions and trainings for skill development and 
awareness. Some of the reputed international brands demand ‘state of art’ machinery and technology 
upgrades to be introduced in the mills, manufacturing their products, to maintain brand competencies 
and quality. The interviews with the managers and CEOs of the large textile facilities indicate that the 
introduction of new and energy efficient, world-class technologies and equipment that are user 
friendly have opened more opportunities and jobs for men and women in textile sector. However, the 
textile mills who have integrated energy efficiency in their operations are only a few large industries, 
while many other due to lack of financial support and expertise, lag much behind. There’s a need of 
financial support to facilitate large-scale adoption of technology upgrades in textile sector to open 
new opportunities for men and women both. 
3.2. 
Perceptions and stereotypes 
Perceptions continue to enhance gender inequality in the sector. Most of the key informants and 
survey respondent shared their perceptions and views regarding the issue.  
The major perceptions/misconceptions for women, that continue to exist are: 
• 
Inability to perform labour intense assignments. The labour-intensive jobs are thus assigned 
to men, reducing the scope of work and wages for women. 
• 
Long, uncertain maternity leaves. Major concern for hiring managers is the long leaves 
women take on account of pregnancy and child care. 
• 
Women are Technologically challenged. Common perception is that the women can’t 
operate on complicated machinery due to their poor understanding of technology, mechanics 
and programming. 
• 
There’s no direct relationship between gender and energy efficiency. Planners often tend to 
overlook the potential of the new, user-friendly energy efficient equipment which will 
facilitate more jobs for women. 
• 
Inefficient to be involved in decision making. In a male dominated sector, participation of 
women in decision making isn’t encouraged. It’s very rare to find women entrepreneurs, MDs 
and CEOs that are actively involved in planning and management. 
• 
Restriction on mobility. Most women in rural regions are discouraged to go beyond the 
boundaries of their village or travel for work. Their mobility should be within their permissive 
boundaries. Barriers on mobility have also reduced work opportunities. 
Women both skilled and unskilled work in mills and perform domestic chores as well. Most of the 
existing misconceptions are led by the patriarchal structure of society which limit women 
empowerment. Training and skill development could break many barriers for women to be better able 
to compete with men and progress financially.

3.3. 
Institutional arrangements (Legal and administrative) 
Women holding the seats of Prime Minister, speaker of parliament, opposition leader and Foreign 
Minister, Bangladeshi women have experienced a great deal of political empowerment. The 
empowering policy, laws and institutions have been discussed below.  
3.3.1. Policy 
Bangladesh’s National Women Development Policy of 201114 mandates to incorporate gender 
perspective in matter of innovation, import and application of new technology. Further, to take 
initiatives to purge the harmful elements, if any, from the technology by conducting research on it if 
the application of such innovated technology affects adversely the interest of the women and make 
necessary legislations, in favour of the women in the field of technology.  
Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan (BCCSAP), 200915 is a 10-year program (2009 – 
2018) of the country to meet the challenge of climate change. BCCSAP addresses the needs of the 
poor and vulnerable, including women and children to be mainstreamed in all the activities under the 
Action Plan. 
Bangladesh Climate Change and Gender Action Plan (ccGAP), 2013 by Ministry of Environment and 
Forest discusses the climate vulnerabilities and gender action plan for four priority areas namely food 
security social protection and health; comprehensive disaster management plan; infrastructure; 
mitigation and low carbon development. 
The National Action Plan16 to prevent violence against women and children (2013-2025) encompasses 
all strategic and practical issues involved in the four pillars of the Women, Peace and Security Agenda: 
Participation, Prevention, Protection, and Relief and Recovery. 
3.3.2. Laws 
The government has undertaken various programs to materialize Vision 202117 for establishing rights 
of women, their empowerment and mainstreaming them in the overall development. Amendments 
have been made to the existing laws and new legislations have been made for protection of women 
and children.  
 Mobile Court Act, 2009:  The executive magistrate was given power to take steps by linking 
Section 509 of the Bangladesh Penal Code in the schedule of Mobile Court Act. This would 
resist and prevent eve teasing and sexual harassment of the girls and women. 
 Citizenship Act (amended), 2009: The provision for giving citizenship by mother to child was 
made by the national parliament by amending the citizenship act in 2009. 
 Dowry Prohibition Act, 1980: Prevention of financial obligation to provide gifts and assets 
 Women and Children Repression Prevention Act, 2000: To ensure the safety and protection 
to women at homes and their surroundings.

 Domestic Violence (Prevention and Protection) Act, 2010: Prevention of domestic

 Prevention and Suppression of Human Trafficking Act, 2012: To prevent trafficking for

 High Court (2009) ruled to include mother’s name in all official documents, school admission

are enhancing vulnerabilities for women due to the lack of climate adaptive capacities.  
 The Bangladesh Labour Act, 2006 amended in 2013: promotes equality of opportunity in 
employment and provides for equal pay amongst men and women.  
 Bangladesh’s Labour Rule, 2015: Mandate for strict rules for misconduct and punishments, 
provident fund, health and fire safety, wages, holiday and bonuses. Especially for women, 
there is a relaxation as the rules prohibits extreme jobs like lifting, carrying or discharging 
weights with hands or head outside the permitted quantity and if pregnant, any of these is 
completely prohibited; Inclusion in the apprenticeship program; provision of separate toilets 
for women; provision of child care or Crèches and consent form for women working in night 
shifts. 
The Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women, 2011 in the Forty-eighth session 
indicated that the persistence of discrimination against women in the labour market, occupational 
segregation, has a wide gender wage gap and the exploitation of girls is also prevalent.18 
3.3.3. Institutions 
The country has significantly progressed by making and adopting integrated gender sensitive national 
policies and institutional arrangements. Since gender is a cross cutting issue, there are a number of 
institutions that are responsible for the recent development in women’s status. Especially, the 
initiatives undertaken by the government institutions like MOWCA19  which is mandated by the 
government to act as a focal point and a catalyst in action for women equality and development 
through different government agencies.

Focal points Mechanism established in 1990 has a designated focal point 
from each ministry to ensure women concerns are integrated in policy, 
plans and programmes, District and Thana level WID coordination 
committees. The WIDFPs of all ministries lead to address gender concerns

In village and Unions, the women have been organized as self-sustained

organization under different government organizations.

Ministry of Finance in 2003, established a monitoring and reporting facility named Recurrent, Capital, 
Gender, and Poverty model which assess the percentage of allocated funds that contribute to poverty 
reduction and gender equity. Women Abuse Prevention Cell and rehabilitation centres for abused 
women have been established to give legal assistance and counselling for prevention of women and 
children abuse. 
Most recently, Bangladesh’s women launched a resistance movement to combat SGBV22 after a series 
of brutal attacks in October 2016. Since the turn of the century, Bangladesh has weathered many 
conflicts with neighbouring states, India and Myanmar, as well as internal conflicts. In addition to 
these conflicts, women still face domestic violence and acid attacks.

3.3.4. Accords and Conventions 
Bangladesh has been actively participating in many international human rights conventions, treaties, 
agreements and instruments and endorsed the Beijing Platform for Action (BPFA). It also adopted 
the United Nations Millennium Declaration (2000) and committed to the goals and targets known as 
the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). Few of the key relevant commitments are listed below. 
 
Chittagong Hill Tracts Peace Accord  
The struggle for promoting the role of women in conflict resolution was a challenge especially in case 
of the Chittagong Hill Tribes region in Bangladesh where many women were widowed during the 
conflicts. Through the peace accord, the regional council gave the opportunity to women to access 
decision-making bodies. The Chittagong Hill Tracts Peace Accord23 also known as Chittagong Hill 
Tracts Treaty, 1997 mandated to have 3 (three) seats for women in every Hill District Council and one-
third of the said seat for non-tribal women. 
Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) 
Bangladesh ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women 
in 1984 and has submitted seven progress reports on its implementation and participated in meetings 
of the Committee on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women. 
ACCORD 
The Accord on Fire and Building Safety in Bangladesh24 helps ensure that no worker needs to fear fire, 
building collapses or other accidents that can be prevented with reasonable health and safety 
measures. This agreement was signed in the immediate aftermath to the Rana Plaza building collapse 
on 24 April 2013, which killed 1,133 workers and critically injured thousands more. The Accord 
protects workers' rights to: 
 Refuse unsafe work 
 Participate in the work of their factory Safety Committee 
 File a complaint when they see a safety problem in their factory 
 Protection against reprisal for reporting safety-related matters 
 Freedom of Association in relation to protecting their own safety 
4. Situational analysis: understanding vulnerabilities through Gender Assessment  
 The situational analysis is based on the real time data collected on field through survey interviews 
with the managers, workers and other stakeholders. 
4.1. 
Socio-economic assessment 
The socio-economic assessment will determine the way the proposed infrastructure development 
programme will affect or change the lives of current or future workforce in textile sector.  
4.1.1. Average age of women in working in textile units 
Approximately, 80 percent25 women workers in textile fall under the age group of 18 to 45 years of 
age.  Most of them tend to leave this sector during mid-40s or early 50s.

4.1.2. Marital status and average household size (adults and children) 
As per the field survey, above 60% of the men and women both in textile labour is married and 
has dependents. Forty five percent survey respondents have an average HH size above 4 
members and the rest of the workers have less than 4 members in the households.

Approximately 60% of the workforce admits that they are given training and orientation before they 
are given responsibility for a task or operations on a machine. Women are given training for skills and 
capacity development on the job as women rarely pursue vocational trainings. While below 10% men 
have undertaken higher/vocational trainings the others undertook trainings at work.

4.1.6. Average work hours 
The survey suggests, there are three, 8 hours shift in medium and large textile units and small textile 
units usually have two, 12 hours shift. Women mostly serve 8 hours shift during day hours.  
As per the Labour Act, No adult worker shall ordinarily work or be required to work in an establishment 
for more than 48 (forty-eight) hours in a week, however, any deviation may happen only as per the 
clauses mentioned. 
4.1.7. Sources of income, average income and expenditure 
Besides their main income source i.e. textile unit, workers that come from farming background make 
money through the agricultural produce, local shops in the area, etc. The analysis from the survey data 
speaks volumes about the disparities in income and wages for men and women (see figure 5). 
 
Figure 5: Gender wise break-up of the monthly income of textile worker group 
The above graph reveals that women worker are paid around 8 to 10% less on average than their male 
compatriots. Lack of skills/technical knowledge and experience, attrition due to unplanned pregnancy, 
selective availability for single day shift, unwillingness to stretch for more hours at work are the most 
frequently listed reason, as per the field survey. Government of Bangladesh through the Labour Rules, 
Labour Law and Women Development Policy have initiated change, but the disparities continue to 
exist in the system.  
4.1.8. Female led households, house ownership and tenancy 
Female led house-holds are those where women provide for the basic needs of the family like buying 
food, clothes, house rents. Apparently, above 30% of the respondent (both men and women) stated 
that women provide for the basic needs of the family which is close to the national statistics26. 
Supplemented saying men invest their income as savings, house maintenance and other agricultural 
farms. Most survey respondents reported that they are living in a rented accommodation away from 
their village. The men and women respondents gave an average estimate of their monthly rents which 
varies from 2,500 BDT to 4,500 BDT a month depending on their requirement (no. of family members 
and assets).

4.1.9. Savings and loans 
According to the Global Findex Database 201727, Bangladesh has approx. 30% gender gap in account 
ownership. Above 90% women and men textile workers do not have a savings and resort to local 
lending in times of needs. The workers that do have savings, have it either in banks or local 
cooperatives. The ease of savings mobilization, availability of savings instruments and the presence 
and intensity of social safety nets determine the savings behaviour.28 As per the responses, 80% men 
and 75% women do not have loans and those who do, have personal loans. Loans for house, vehicle 
and educated hasn’t been reported.  
4.1.10. Provident fund/life Insurance 
All the respondents stated that their employers do not give or facilitate provident fund opportunity. 
The respondents, either men or women do not have any kind of insurance either personal or provided 
by the employer. Recently, the labour rules have mandated the provision of Provident Fund by the 
employer and laid out clauses for the same and also for the obligatory group insurance policy by the 
employer. 
4.2. 
Health and Safety assessment 
4.2.1. Health issues faced by men and women in textile units 
Its known that the textile units have a low air quality index and a high suspended particulate matter 
which is suffocating and in the long-term lead to bronchial diseases. Besides, there’s heat and 
chemicals in the work premises that the employs work with.29 As reported by the workers, there are 
frequent instances of burning from the heated machinery and effluents and chemical reactions by the 
dyes and colours. 
4.2.2. Health camps and medical facilities (like dispensary) and health insurance 
Men and women in large industries reported that their employers conduct health camps in association 
with government and private hospitals. Similarly, it was reported that there are 24*7 functional 
dispensaries in the large units that are looked after by government certified doctors. While the 
workers in small and medium textile units reported that there are no health camps or in-house medical 
facilities made available by their employer and they are directed to local medical practitioner in the 
vicinity. 
In case of accidents and injuries at workplace in larger units, if the damage cannot be handled by a 
resident doctor, the victims are rushed to the nearby medical facility and the finances are covered by 
the employer.  
The respondents shared there are no health insurance being provided by the employers.  
4.2.3. Maternity leaves 
Respondents shared that there is a provision of maternity leaves for 2 to 4 months and the 
Government also has legalised the maternity leave for 6 months and the female workers can also opt 
for maternity benefit during their absence. However, there isn’t provision for paternity leaves. 
4.2.4. Violence and abuse at work 
Men and women responded of not having been abused at work in large and medium units, however, 
in smaller units a few instances of verbal abuse have been reported. Most of the instances of verbal 
 
27 World Bank Global Findex 2017 (https://globalfindex.worldbank.org/)

abuse have been reported due to work related issues and incompetency to work on complex 
machine systems or carry out extreme tasks like carry, shift, load, the material.  
The Labour Rules 2015 has prescribed a limited load quantity to be carried by both men and women 
and anything over and above that is an offence as per the rules. 
4.2.5. Injuries and Accidents  
Of the total respondents, 25% of women and men did mention of having experienced an injury at work 
while 15 % women and 25% men shared their experiences of having witnessed accidents and injuries 
due to fire or other occupational hazards like heated boilers, hot dyeing machines and hot dyed 
material taken off the dyeing machines for cooling and finishing, chemical dyes, generators and 
electric equipment, Switch boards while power on/off and much more. The cost of damage is mostly 
covered by the employer if the accident/injury takes place at workplace. The Labour Rules has set 
rules in favour of the workers where they/their dependents can seek money from the employer 
through the Labour Court. Also, The owner shall place Material Safety Data Sheet (MSDS) of dangerous 
materials in an easily noticeable place so that the employed worker can be well informed about the 
possible hazards. 
4.2.6. Safety precautions, trainings and tools 
Typical risks in a textile factory have previously been discussed. To prevent accidents and injuries and 
reduce the intensity of disaster, there are specific guidelines that needs to be followed while operating 
on machines, doing manual labour, dealing with electricity or heat.  
There needs to be protection for respiratory health, eyes, fingers and limbs, for which the employer 
must mandatorily provide with necessary safety attire like safety shoes, helmets, goggles, masks, 
hand gloves, ear muffs, ear plugs, waist belts, aprons etc. and arrange training programs for the 
workers in using these materials and ensure their usage. Also, Employment of children, teenage 
laborers in the activities that are declared as dangerous from time to time by the Inspector General 
or his representative is prohibited30.

4.3. 
Sanitation and access to basic facilities 
4.3.1. Access to drinking water, washrooms and sanitation facilities (soaps, sanitary napkins, etc) 
As per the site visits, the drinking water available for the workers are from the government’s water 
pipeline being provided for the locality. The size of the textile units determines if the water is further 
processed by in-house water filters. Out of 20 units that were visited 17 units had water purification 
facility.  
Similarly, hygiene and washroom facilities are dependent on the size of the textile units. The larger 
the set-up and workforce, the better facilities are provided. As observed, separate washrooms are 
provided if the facilities are medium to large scale and have a bigger workforce.  
However, Labour Rules state ‘there will be provision of pure drinking water in easily accessible and 
suitable place for all workers in each firm and the water should be stored in a hygienic way’ and lays 
down rules for Toilets and washrooms- the number, location and cleanliness of the toilets and wash 
room should be in accordance and in case of female workers, one toilet for each 25 females up to the 
first 100 and one for each 50 next.

Evidently, presence of women in the sector is very less. Only a few women have been reported to be leading the textile units as Managing Directors and CEOs. 
There is a dire need to address inequalities being experienced by the respective genders in different cases. The development programmes in textile sector in 
Bangladesh must address gender issues for growth and social welfare.

6. Bridging Gaps: gender sensitivity and applicability of the programme 
As per the GCF’s Gender Policy, the following must be considered for the Gender Action Plan 
intended to  
6.1. 
Inclusivity 
in 
stakeholder 
approach 
(planning, 
management 
and 
implementation) 
Infrastructure development programmes with the GCF have a mandate to have a gender sensitive 
approach and gender inclusion in all activities including stakeholder engagement and consultations to 
operationalise fair and balanced programme actions. Since, there has been a proactive engagement 
of the stakeholders both men and women in planning and designing (consultations and surveys) of 
the programme, the existing issues in textile sector could surface. 
6.1.1. First stakeholder workshop 
A gender balanced scoping workshop on ‘Importance of energy efficiency in textile sector and its 
influence on environment, society and gender’ was conducted at Dhaka on 29th May 2019 to engage 
with stakeholders from ministries, associations, industry and civil society organisations and discuss 
issues related to gender parity in the sector. Respective stakeholders could communicate their 
concerns about the status and the impact of programme through various phases (planning, designing, 
and implementation). The discussions on average male female ratio, roles of gender in management 
and decision-making, the trends in perceptions and attitudes and enabling change for a balanced 
approached, paved way for an integrated programme that’s socially benefitting to the vulnerable 
group. 
6.1.2. Second stakeholder workshop  
A validation cum consultation workshop for dissemination of the findings of the gender assessment 
done through various stakeholder engagement methods (field survey, worker interviews, manager 
interviews, policy makers interviews, etc) was held titled ‘Impacts of Energy Efficiency on 
Environment, Social and Gender in Textile Sector of Bangladesh’ on 29th July 2019 in Dhaka.  Most 
of the distinguished stakeholders from ministries, associations, trade unions, industries, academicians 
and development professionals participated in the workshop to state views and comment on the 
observations and conclusions of the exercise.  
 
6.2. 
Monitoring interventions and Evaluation  
Gender sensitive vision and objectives has led to specific log-frame indicators to embed gender 
inclusion in programme design. The M&E log frame will measure the performance of the gender-based 
activities. The action plan will present:

Enhanced livelihood opportunities and reduced inequalities at work through training and skill development to improve the productivity and competitiveness for 
both men and women from the implementation of energy efficient technologies and equipment in textile sector. 
Outcome Statement:  
Increased access of the textile sector to concessional financing for energy efficiency, leading to energy savings through large scale adoption of energy efficient 
technologies to reduce GHG emissions.
